**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** records, with three chains from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī, the Prophet's ﷺ distinction: "As for the people of the Fire who are its people — they do not die in it nor live. But there are people whom the Fire strikes because of their sins — God causes them truly to die, until they become coals; then permission for intercession is given" (Muslim) [Ṭabarī]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** adds the matching verses from Ṭā-Hā: "whoever follows My guidance neither goes astray nor is wretched" (20:123) and "whoever turns away from My remembrance — his is a life of constriction, and We shall raise him blind on the Day of Resurrection" (20:124) — with Ibn ʿAbbās's gloss: not misguided in this life, not wretched in the next.

**Cross-references.** 20:123–124; 2:38, the promise immediately before; 2:6 and 2:8, the two groups the warning is carried forward to [Ṭabarī].

**Reflection.** Two verses, two ends, and no third: the one who follows guidance loses nothing that matters, and the one who denies the signs keeps nothing that lasts. The Adam story does not end with the Garden lost; it ends with a fork in the road of the earth, and the reader standing where Adam stood — on a borrowed resting place, "for a while", with the promise of guidance already given [Saʿdī] [Ṭabarī].


## 2:40

*"O Children of Israel, remember My favour which I bestowed upon you, and fulfil My covenant and I shall fulfil your covenant, and be in awe of Me."*

**Meaning.** The sūrah opens a new section — the longest in it — addressing the people of the Book in Madīnah and around it, and it opens gently: by the honour of their ancestry, by the favours done to their forebears, and by a bargain: keep the covenant and God keeps His [Maʿārif] [Saʿdī]. **[Qurṭubī]** notes the tone: God binds the Children of Israel to remembering the *favour*, while He called the community of Muḥammad to remembering *Him* — "Remember Me, I shall remember you" (2:152). The earlier peoples began from the gift and were led to the Giver; this community begins with the Giver and is led to the gift.

**Context.** "Israel" is the name of Yaʿqūb b. Isḥāq b. Ibrāhīm: from Ibn ʿAbbās, *Isrā* in Hebrew is "servant" and *Īl* is God, so *Isrāʾīl* is "the servant of God" [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī], and in the Prophet's own exchange with a group of Jews, recorded by Abū Dāwūd al-Ṭayālisī: "Do you know that Israel is Yaʿqūb?" — "Yes, by God" — "O God, be witness" [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Qurṭubī]** notes it is a foreign name, hence not declined, and lists its seven pronunciations in the readings, and its other meanings: the servant of God, God's chosen one, or — al-Suhaylī — the one who travelled by night (*asrā*) when he migrated to God. **[Ibn Kathīr]** explains why the address names the ancestor: it is the appeal of lineage to the son of a righteous father — "O son of the brave man, engage the brave" — the way 17:3 says, "O offspring of those We carried with Nūḥ; surely he was a grateful servant."

**The favours.** **[Ṭabarī]** gathers what the *niʿmah* was. Abū al-ʿĀliyah: that God made prophets and messengers from among them and sent down Books to them. Mujāhid: that He split the rock for them, sent down manna and quails, and saved them from the servitude of Pharaoh's house. Ibn ʿAbbās: His favours upon them and their fathers — what He saved them from at Pharaoh's hand. Ibn Zayd: the favours are general, and no favour is greater than Islam; the rest follow after it, as 49:17 reads, "They count it a favour to you that they have submitted; say: do not count your submission as a favour to me; rather God has favoured you in guiding you to faith." And **[Qurṭubī]** makes the point that binds the whole passage: God's favours to the fathers are favours to the sons, because the sons are honoured by the fathers' honour — and among what their forefathers were entrusted with was the Torah, in which were the description of Muhammad ﷺ and his prophethood. **[Study Quran]** takes the *niʿmah* in the widest sense — beginning with creation itself — which is why the commands that follow are universal in scope.

**The covenant.** **[Ṭabarī]** identifies the covenant here as what God took upon them in the Torah: that they would make plain to the people that Muḥammad ﷺ is God's Messenger, whom they find written with them; that they would believe in him and in what came to him. Their side: faith and obedience. God's side: what He promised in return — the Garden, as 5:12 sets out: "God took the covenant of the Children of Israel, and We raised from them twelve chieftains, and God said: I am with you if you establish the prayer and give charity and believe in My messengers and support them and lend God a goodly loan — I shall expiate your evil deeds, and I shall admit you to Gardens beneath which rivers run." And 7:156–157: "I shall ordain it for those who are reverent and give charity and believe in Our signs, who follow the Messenger, the unlettered prophet, whom they find written down with them in the Torah and the Gospel." Ibn ʿAbbās, al-Suddī, al-Ḍaḥḥāk, Abū al-ʿĀliyah, al-Rabīʿ and Ibn Zayd agree that the promised fulfilment is Paradise and God's good pleasure [Ṭabarī] [Ibn Kathīr] [Qurṭubī]. **[Qurṭubī]** reports the many glosses: the covenant of "take what We have given you with strength" (2:63), the twelve chieftains of 5:12, the 3:187 covenant to make the Book known and not conceal it, and al-Zajjāj's plain reading — believe in what God covenanted with you in the Torah concerning Muḥammad ﷺ, and God will give you what He promised. The majority hold the widest sense: the covenant covers all its commands, prohibitions and charges, and the mention of Muḥammad's description in the Torah is one of them. And he adds the reminder that what was demanded of these people is demanded of us: "Fulfil the contracts" (5:1), "Fulfil the covenant of God" (16:91) — and the fulfilment of God's covenant to them was pure grace, not an obligation owed.

**Language.** *Dhikr* is shared between heart and tongue: remembrance of the heart is the opposite of forgetfulness, of the tongue the opposite of silence — and the sense here is to remember the favour with gratitude: "remember My favour" is taken as "remember My favour with thanks", the mention of the benefit standing in for the thanks it deserves [Qurṭubī]. Reading *ishrāʾīl*: an oblique name with the *yāʾ* dropped in the vocative [Qurṭubī]. And on the *yāʾ* of "be in awe of Me" (*fa-irhabūni*): it falls away at the end of the āyah because the verse closes; Ibn Abī Isḥāq read it with the *yāʾ* — *fa-irhabūni* — as the origin requires, and similarly *fa-ittaqūni* [Qurṭubī]. **[Qurṭubī]** also notes the reading of al-Zuhrī: *ūfī* with a doubled *fāʾ*, "I shall fulfil abundantly."

**Rulings.** Two injunctions the commentators draw from the verse itself [Maʿārif] [Qurṭubī]. First, the obligation of keeping faith and agreements: the command to fulfil is universal, the verse is explicit, and 5:1 confirms it; the ḥadīth in Muslim adds the warning — that those who break their promises will be marked out before all mankind on the Day of Judgement by a flag, its size matching the gravity of the breach. Second, the ordering of the address: God began with encouragement and lineage, and closed with *fear* — *be in awe of Me* — and no one else; the Prophet's ﷺ own words to the Israelites of Madīnah drew the same line: they were being asked to speak and believe the truth about him and feared the loss of their income and status if they did. **[Ibn Kathīr]** reads the verse as encouragement followed by warning so that they might return, follow the Messenger, heed the Qurʾān's commands and believe in its contents.

**Reflection.** **[Maʿārif]** sets out the shape of this whole long section: among the disbelievers of the Prophet's day were the idolaters of Makkah, largely unlettered, and the People of the Book — respected, trusted, and capable of carrying others with them by their example. The sūrah, being Madinan, speaks to the second group at length, and it starts by appealing to their nobility and their history before it argues. That is the pastoral intelligence of the verse: to a people who could lose everything by saying yes, God says first: *remember*. Remember what your houses were built on. Remember who your father was. Remember what was done for you before you were asked for anything — and then, on the basis of all that memory, be in awe of no one but the One whose favour runs through your whole history.


## 2:41

*"And believe in what I have sent down, confirming what is with you; and be not the first to disbelieve in it; and do not sell My signs for a paltry price — and be in awe of Me."*

**Meaning.** The command tightens around the People of the Book: the Book that has come is a confirmation of what they already hold, so faith in it is not a departure from their own scriptures but obedience to them [Ṭabarī] [Saʿdī]. **[Saʿdī]** puts the argument plainly: the Qurʾān agrees with what you have and contradicts it in nothing, so nothing prevents you from believing; rather, you are the first who should believe, being the people of the Book and of knowledge. And 7:157 has already said the Messenger is written with them.

**Context — "confirming what is with you".** **[Ṭabarī]** explains: the Qurʾān confirms the Torah, and faith in the Qurʾān is by that very fact faith in the Torah, since what the Qurʾān commands — acknowledging Muḥammad's prophethood, believing and following him — is the like of what is in the Torah and the Gospel. So in believing in what came down to Muḥammad they believe in what is with them; and in denying it they deny what is with them. Abū al-ʿĀliyah: "O People of the Book, believe in what I sent down to Muḥammad confirming what you have — for they find Muḥammad written in the Torah and the Gospel." **[Study Quran]** notes the two ways "confirming" is taken: that it attests the validity of the earlier books and their teachings, or that it fulfils their foretelling of the Prophet's mission — on the second reading the verse becomes an imperative implied by their own scriptures.

**Context — "the first to disbelieve".** The exegetes asked how a plural address can be told not to be "the first *kāfir*" — a singular noun. **[Ṭabarī]** gives the grammar: *afʿal* used as a noun derived from a verb stands in for *man* ("the first *of those who* disbelieve"), so it renders the sense of the plural while remaining one word — as one says "the army is routed" and "the troops are advancing", keeping the predicate singular for the singular word though the sense is many. He notes the poet's two lines that do both in one breath: "and when they eat, the meanest of eaters; and when they are hungry, the worst of the hungry" — singular in one place, plural in the other, and both correct. As to the pronoun of "in it": Ibn Jurayj said the Qurʾān; Abū al-ʿĀliyah said Muḥammad ﷺ; a third group said their own book. **[Ṭabarī]** prefers the Qurʾān, because what the verse commands faith in at its beginning is the Book, and the natural reference of the pronoun at its end is the same object. **[Qurṭubī]** adds: those already disbelieving in Makkah had preceded them, so "first" means first of the People of the Book — for they are the ones whose judgement and knowledge others look to in such matters. **[Ibn Kathīr]** cites the explanation from Ibn ʿAbbās: do not be the first to deny it while you have more knowledge of it than others [Ibn Kathīr]. And why the emphasis on *first*? **[Maʿārif]** says the man who leads others into denial carries not only his own sin but the sin of everyone who follows his example — bringing multiple punishment — just as one who opens a good door has a share in the good of those who walk through it. **[Study Quran]** adds that "being the first" in Qurʾānic usage is a mark of priority and quality rather than mere sequence: "I was commanded to be the first of those who submit" (6:14, 6:163; 7:143).

**Rulings — "do not sell My signs for a paltry price".** **[Ṭabarī]** reports Abū al-ʿĀliyah: do not take payment for it — and it stands written in the first Book, "O son of Adam, teach freely as you were taught freely." Al-Suddī: do not take a covetous price. **[Qurṭubī]** collects what the rabbis were doing: taking bribes to alter the description of Muḥammad ﷺ in the Book; living off fixed allowances attached to their learning; teaching their religion for a fee. He then draws the rule that reaches far beyond their case: the verse, though addressed to the Children of Israel, covers whoever does their deed — one who takes a bribe to alter a right, or withholds teaching that is obligatory on him until he is paid for it, has fallen under the verse's meaning. The ḥadīth of Abū Hurayrah in Abū Dāwūd: "Whoever learns a knowledge by which God's face is sought, learning it only for some worldly thing, will not find the scent of Paradise on the Day of Resurrection." Then he gives the jurists' long debate about wages for teaching the Qurʾān. Al-Zuhrī and the people of opinion forbade it, arguing that teaching is among the obligations that require sincerity and nearness to God and so cannot be sold, like prayer and fasting. Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, Aḥmad, Abū Thawr and most of the scholars permitted it, on the Prophet's ﷺ word in the ḥadīth of the healing recitation: "The worthiest thing for which you take a wage is the Book of God" (al-Bukhārī) — a clear text that should settle the difference; and the analogy from prayer is unsound, because prayer is an act confined to the one performing it, while teaching the Qurʾān passes its benefit to another. *(weak)* He also cites the report from Ibn ʿAbbās, "The teachers of your children are the worst of you, the least merciful to the orphan and the harshest to the poor," and Abū Hurayrah's "Their dirham is forbidden, their clothing ill-gotten, their speech hypocrisy" — and notes that the scholars of transmission judged these chains defective: Saʿīd b. Ṭarīf, the narrator of the first, is abandoned; Abū Jurhum, in the second, is unknown, and the report has no basis; the ḥadīth of ʿUbādah b. al-Ṣāmit about the bow has its own defects, though it is better known. And the answers to the verse: it concerns the Children of Israel, and the law of those before us is not necessarily our law; or it concerns the man on whom teaching is obligatory and who refuses until paid; as for one not so obligated, the Sunnah permits him to take a wage. And when Abū Bakr was made caliph he took his cloth to the market, and when it was said to him he replied, "Then from where shall I feed my family?" — so they returned him and assigned him his sufficiency [Qurṭubī].

**Reflection.** **[Maʿārif]** looks at the sequence of the two prohibitions: do not be the first to deny, and do not sell My signs. The first is about leading — the scholar whose denial teaches a whole community to deny; the second is about the price — the scholar who bends the Book to keep his income. Both target the same person, and both warn about the same thing: a person of knowledge whom knowledge does not save. Later jurists, he notes, allowed wages for teaching when the state's support of teachers collapsed and the transmission of the Qurʾān itself was at stake — but limited the permission to genuine need; and treating recitation as a paid service to convey reward to the dead, he says, citing al-Shāmī, is a practice never reported from the Companions or their successors.


## 2:42

*"And do not mix the truth with falsehood, nor conceal the truth while you know."*

**Meaning.** Two prohibitions for the people of the Book, and for anyone who inherits their situation: do not blur the true with the false so that the seeker cannot tell them apart, and do not hide the truth you know [Ibn Kathīr] [Saʿdī]. **[Saʿdī]** puts it as the two duties of the people of knowledge — distinguishing the truth and making it plain. God has set out His signs and made His proofs clear precisely so the truth may be separated from falsehood and the way of the guided from the way of the guilty; whoever does this is among the successors of the messengers and the guides of nations, and whoever blurs and conceals is a caller to Hell.

**Language.** The root is *labasa*, to mix: *labastu ʿalayhi al-amr* is to blend the clear of a matter with its confusion, its truth with its falsehood — as 6:9 says, "and We would have confused them in what they confuse." In a matter there is *lubsah*, a confusion, when it is not clear. From this came ʿAlī's word to al-Ḥārith b. Ḥawṭ: "O Ḥārith, it is confused for you; the truth is not known by men — know the truth and you will know its people." The poetess al-Khansāʾ warns the same: "you see the companion speak a truth and think it guidance, but be careful what he has mixed in; believe his word and beware his hostility, and clothe yourself against him as he has clothed." And *labasa* also carries the sense of wearing clothing — the *libās* of a man is his wife — so some read the verse as "do not cover the truth"; ʿAntara's line "a squadron I clothed with a squadron" is from either sense [Qurṭubī]. *Bāṭil* is the opposite of truth: "everything but God is vanity" [Qurṭubī]. On the grammar of *wa-taktumū l-ḥaqq*: it may be joined to "mix" and so take the jussive — "do not mix and do not conceal" — or it may be put in the accusative by an implied *an*, "and that you conceal it": either way the prohibition is plain [Qurṭubī].

**Context — what was mixed, what was hidden.** **[Ṭabarī]** works the grammar through and reaches the meaning: O rabbis of the People of the Book, do not confuse the people about Muḥammad ﷺ and what he brought from his Lord — do not claim he was sent to some nations and not to others, or adopt two faces in his affair, when you know he was sent to all of you and to all nations besides you; by that you mix the true with the untrue, and hide what you find in your Book of his description: that he is My messenger to mankind entire, and that what came to you from him is from Me. The reports on the concealment are unanimous: Abū al-ʿĀliyah and Mujāhid — they concealed the news of Muḥammad's mission while finding him written with them in the Torah and the Gospel; al-Suddī — "the truth" here is Muḥammad ﷺ himself; Ibn ʿAbbās — they concealed what they had of knowledge of God's Messenger and what he brought, finding it in the Books in their hands [Ṭabarī]. **[Qurṭubī]** collects the readings of what the mixture was: Ibn ʿAbbās and others — do not mix the truth that is in the Book with falsehood, that is, do not change it and substitute; Abū al-ʿĀliyah — the Jews said, "Muḥammad is indeed sent, but to others, not to us": their acknowledgment that he was sent was true and their denial that he was sent to *them* was false; Ibn Zayd — the truth is the Torah and the falsehood is what they substituted in it; Mujāhid and Qatāda — do not mix Judaism and Christianity with Islam while knowing that God's religion, which accepts nothing else, is Islam. **[Qurṭubī]** prefers Ibn ʿAbbās's because it is general and includes all the rest. And he reports Muḥammad b. Sīrīn's account of how the concealment happened: a group of the descendants of Hārūn settled at Yathrib when the Children of Israel suffered defeat and humiliation; they were the bearers of the Torah in their day, and they settled there hoping that Muḥammad would appear among them, believing and affirming his prophethood. The fathers passed away in belief, and the sons and grandsons succeeded them, and when they found Muḥammad ﷺ they denied him — while knowing him.

**Rulings.** **[Maʿārif]** reads the verse as a direct ruling: it is not permitted to mix truth with falsehood so that the hearer is left in confusion about what the truth is, and it is not permitted to conceal the truth out of fear or greed. To illustrate the second, he gives the long report from al-Dārimī's *Musnad* of Abū Ḥāzim — the man who had sat with a Companion — brought before the Umayyad caliph Sulaymān b. ʿAbd al-Malik. The caliph asked why he had not come to visit him; Abū Ḥāzim answered that the caliph had not known his name before that day nor he ever seen the caliph's face, and al-Zuhrī said, "Abū Ḥāzim is right, and you are wrong." Asked why he disliked death, he said, "Because you have made this world flourish and made your home in the next a desert — and a man does not like to leave a flourishing place for a desert." Asked how they would appear before God, he said: the doer of good, like a traveller returning to his loved ones; the doer of evil, like a runaway slave brought back to his master. Which verse? "The righteous shall be in bliss, and the wicked in a blazing fire" (82:13–14) — and when the caliph said God's mercy is vast, he recited, "Truly God's mercy is near to those who do good" (7:56). Asked who is the noblest of God's servants, he said: those mindful of others and of sound understanding. Asked what justice the ruler owes, he reminded him of how his forefathers took rule by the sword and departed the world — and when a courtier rebuked him, he said, "I said nothing rude; it is what God commanded us, for He charged the scholars to make the Book clear and not conceal it" (3:187). When the caliph sent him a hundred dinars, Abū Ḥāzim returned them with a letter: if this is a wage for my words, then blood and pork are cleaner than it; and if it is my due from the treasury, there are hundreds of scholars and servants of Islam — send the same to each of them, or keep it. And **[Maʿārif]** draws the point: refusing to be paid for counsel shows that taking wages for an act of worship or obedience is not permissible.

**Reflection.** The two prohibitions belong together: concealment is the practical form of mixing, and mixing is the respectable name for concealment. A scholar who knows the truth and hides it has not merely kept silent — he has altered the map others will use. **[Qurṭubī]**'s chain of generations at Yathrib is the anatomy of it: fathers who believed and waited, sons who found what the fathers waited for and denied it while knowing it; the truth was not lost by the children for lack of evidence but because something else had come to weigh more than evidence. And the verse's last words — "while you know" — leave the reader with no shelter: the sin it forbids cannot be committed in ignorance.


## 2:43

*"And establish the prayer, and give the alms, and bow with those who bow."*

**Meaning.** The commands turn from doctrine to deed, and to the two deeds that carry the rest: the prayer that binds the servant to God, and the alms that bind him to men [Maʿārif] [Ṭabarī]. Then the third clause names the shape of it — *bow with those who bow*: do not worship alone in your own manner; enter the community of those who submit [Ibn Kathīr] [Qurṭubī].

**Context.** **[Ṭabarī]** reports the occasion Qatāda and others give: the rabbis of the Jews and the hypocrites used to order people to establish the prayer and give the alms while not doing it themselves — so God commanded them to establish the prayer *with the believers* in Muḥammad and what he brought, to pay the alms from their wealth with them, and to humble themselves to God and His Messenger as they had humbled themselves. It is a command to enter the fold and act within it, not to advise others from outside. **[Jalālayn]** gives the related report: the verse came down concerning their scholars, who used to tell their relatives among the Muslims, "Stay firm on the religion of Muḥammad, for it is the truth" — while keeping to their own ways. And **[Ibn Kathīr]** gives Muqātil's reading of the three clauses: establish the prayer *behind the Prophet* ﷺ; give the alms *to the Prophet*; bow with those who bow *among the community of Muḥammad* — meaning, be with the believers and among them.

**Language.** "Establish the prayer" carries more than "perform" it: it is to set it upright, keep its times and conditions, and hold it standing [Ṭabarī]. *Ītāʾ* — giving — is the delivery of the obligatory charity to those entitled to it; and the root of *zakāh* is growth and increase: the crop *zakā* when what God brings out of it multiplies; a single object *zakā* when a second is added and it becomes a pair, as the poet says of the party "they were either odd or even below four"; and a man is *zakī*, clean of sins, as Mūsā says of the soul, "Have you killed a pure soul?" (18:74) [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī]. **[Ṭabarī]** says the second derivation pleases him more — that the charity is called *zakāh* because it purifies what remains of a man's wealth and clears it from the wrong that the shares of the poor had in it. **[Qurṭubī]** gives the same two roots and adds a third: *zakāh* as fair reputation, from *tazkiyat al-shāhid* — as if the giver obtains a good name for himself. And the Prophet ﷺ called what is taken out in charity "the filth of the people" — "take from their wealth a charity by which you purify them and cause them increase" (9:103). As for *rukūʿ*: it is lowliness and submission — to bow to a thing is to submit to it; the poet's she-camel "cried out for help after it had bowed", meaning after it had given in to exhaustion [Ṭabarī].

**Rulings.** **[Qurṭubī]** gives the *zakāh* here as the obligatory charity, since it is joined with the prayer; Mālik is reported to have held it to mean the charity of *ʿĪd* (zakāt al-fiṭr), and Qurṭubī answers that most scholars hold the first, and that the Book's *zakāh* is stated in general terms which the Prophet ﷺ detailed: from Abū Saʿīd, "There is no charity in grain or dates until it reaches five camel-loads, nor in fewer than five camels, nor in fewer than five ounces of silver" (al-Bukhārī); and from Ibn ʿUmar, "In what the sky and the springs water — or what is rain-fed — a tenth; and in what is watered by drawing, a twentieth" [Qurṭubī] [Ibn Kathīr]. And on "bow with those who bow", **[Qurṭubī]**'s great chapter on the prayer — he counts thirty-four questions — turns on this phrase as the proof of congregation: the obligation of praying together, the rules of the imām and the follower, the rows and the leadership, since the verse commands them to bow *with* the bowers, not merely to bow. **[Ibn Kathīr]** likewise says many scholars took the verse as evidence for the obligation of congregation, and **[Study Quran]** notes that bowing is a universal gesture of worship and reverence, defined in Islam as a specific posture of the canonical prayer. And on the universality of the two acts: **[Study Quran]** observes that prayer and alms were commanded to earlier peoples too — Ismāʿīl "used to command his people to pray and give alms" (19:55), and 19:31 and 20:14 likewise — so the commands are not a novel imposition but the renewal of an old one.

**Reflection.** **[Maʿārif]** diagnoses why these two commands follow the appeal to the People of the Book: what kept the scholars from accepting Islam was, as a rule, the love of money and power. Against money, patience — *ṣabr*, in its three senses: bearing hardship, restraining oneself from sin, and standing firm in obedience; against ambition, the prayer, which is humility inside and out. He draws the psychological line: money is sought only to feed appetites, so a man who resolves not to be a slave of his appetites loses his need for much of it; and the prayer trains humility until pride has nothing to stand on. Then he answers the obvious objection — that the prayer he prescribes is itself hard, five times a day, with its renunciation of even lawful things at fixed hours. The heart, he says, loves to move freely in thought and fancy; as long as it keeps moving, the limbs must serve its motion, and the fixed hours of prayer are a discipline aimed exactly at that motion. So the remedy for a restless heart is rest — *khushūʿ*, which he translates not merely as humility but as "the restfulness of the heart" — and since the mind cannot move in two directions at once, the Qurʾān gives the heart one thought to absorb it so that the others fall away of themselves. And the community is part of the cure: the command is not "bow", but "bow *with those who bow*" — the discipline is easier among people who are doing it [Maʿārif] [Qurṭubī]. The verse thus closes the first stretch of the address not with belief alone but with the two hands of religion: one raised to God, one open to the poor, and both offered in the company of the believers.


## 2:44

*"Will you command people to righteousness and forget your own selves, while you recite the Book? Will you not understand?"*

**Meaning.** The verse turns the argument on the scholars themselves: you teach the good and neglect your own souls — and you are people who *read the Book* [Saʿdī] [Jalālayn]. **[Saʿdī]** says the reproach is precisely a charge of not thinking: the word *ʿaql* means to tether, and reason is named for it because a man ties with it what benefits him and holds himself back from what harms him. The first person a man's good counsel should reach is himself; a man who orders others to good and does not do it, or forbids evil and does not leave it, has shown his own lack of reason — especially when he *knows*, and the proof has risen against him.

**Context.** **[Ṭabarī]** reports the interpretations, and notes that they converge though they differ in wording. From Ibn ʿAbbās: you forbid people the disbelief that is in your Book's covenant, and leave yourselves — you disbelieve in what is in it of My covenant that you must believe in My Messenger, and you break My pact and deny what you know of My Book. From al-Suddī and Qatāda: they used to order the people to the obedience of God and reverence for Him, and they opposed Him themselves — so God exposed them. From Ibn Jurayj: the People of the Book and the hypocrites used to order people to fast and pray and then not act on what they ordered, so God reproached them — and the lesson he draws: whoever commands a good thing should be the swiftest of people to it. From Ibn Zayd, the detail that shows the crookedness of it: when a man came asking them something in which there was no right (or bribe) for them, they would order him to the truth — and God said to them, *Will you command people to righteousness and forget your own selves?* **[Ṭabarī]** concludes that the verse, according to its plain sense, means: will you command people to the obedience of God and leave yourselves disobeying Him? Why do you not order your own souls to do what you order others? — reproaching them and making their conduct ugly to them. And "forgetting themselves" here is the forgetting of 9:67 — "they forgot God, so He forgot them": they abandoned, so they were abandoned. *(weak)* From Abū Qilābah, from Abū al-Dardāʾ: a man does not reach full understanding until he hates people for God's sake, then turns to himself and hates himself more. The chain reaching Abū al-Dardāʾ is discontinuous — Abū Qilābah's death is decades after his — but the saying is cited as wisdom [Ṭabarī, editor].

**Language.** *Al-birr*: **[Ṭabarī]** notes the scholars agree that every act of obedience to God is called *birr*, and they differ only over which one the verse has in view. **[Study Quran]** gives the range: goodness, kindness, loyalty, sincerity, obedience — with 2:177, 2:189, 3:92 for the term, and the *abrār* receiving heavenly rewards (3:193; 76:5; 82:13). "You recite the Book" — *tatlūna* is to read and to study it; the Book here is the Torah [Ṭabarī] [Study Quran]. *Afalā taʿqilūn* — will you not understand — is Ibn ʿAbbās's gloss: will you not see the ugliness of disobeying God in the very thing you command others to do, when you know that the same duty binds you as binds those you command? [Ṭabarī]

**Rulings.** **[Saʿdī]** guards the verse from a misuse it invites. It is not a licence to stop commanding good or forbidding evil; the verse rebukes a man in relation to *two* duties, and there are indeed two: commanding others, and commanding oneself. Leaving one is not permission to leave the other — perfection is to do both, the worst is to do neither, and the man who does one alone is between the two. And he adds the practical reason the verse cares: people are formed not to follow a man whose deeds contradict his words; they are moved by example more than by bare speech. **[Study Quran]** notes the same generalizing: some read the verse as aimed specifically at acknowledging the Prophet's mission, or at the warning to the Arabs about a coming prophet, and the language of *birr* in it covers prayer and alms and obedience broadly.

**Reflection.** **[Saʿdī]** anchors the verse in the community that came after: the Scripture it echoes is 61:2–3, "O you who believe, why do you say what you do not do? It is most hateful with God that you say what you do not do." The address has changed, the disease has not. And the question it ends on — *will you not understand?* — is the sharpest form of the reproach, because it does not accuse the scholar of ignorance. He has read the Book; he knows the standard; the whole indictment is that he can see it clearly enough to teach it to someone else.


## 2:45

*"And seek help in patience and prayer — and indeed it is heavy, except for the humble."*

**Meaning.** The command that follows the reproach of 2:44 is the remedy: the burdens that keep men from faith — appetite and the love of position — are to be met with *ṣabr* and *ṣalāh* [Maʿārif] [Jalālayn]. **[Ṭabarī]** puts the occasion directly: seek help, O rabbis of the People of the Book, by holding your souls to the obedience of God and restraining them from His disobedience, and by establishing the prayer — the prayer that forbids indecency and wrong, brings near to what pleases God, and is *heavy* to establish except for those who humble themselves before God and submit to Him in fear.

**Language.** *Ṣabr* in Arabic is holding back: one says "so-and-so was killed *ṣabran*" — held and detained until he was destroyed; "I made myself patient over a thing" is to imprison myself against it; and the *maṣbūrah*, the animal forbidden in the ḥadīth, is the one tied up to die [Qurṭubī] [Ṭabarī]. From the same root the poet speaks of the free woman who "was patient, knowing" while the coward's soul looked over the edge [Qurṭubī]. And *khushūʿ* is lowliness, submission and stillness: Jerīr's line over al-Zubayr — "when the news came, the wall of the city bowed, and the humbled mountains." **[Ṭabarī]** gives the reports on the *khāshiʿīn*: the humble before His obedience, the fearful of His power, the affirming of His promise and warning [Ṭabarī]; Ibn ʿAbbās — those who affirm what God sent down; Abū al-ʿĀliyah — the fearful; Mujāhid — truly the believers; Ibn Zayd — *khushūʿ* is fear and awe, as 42:45 has them "humbled by disgrace" [Ṭabarī]. *Kabīrah* here is heavy and burdensome: al-Ḍaḥḥāk — "it is a weight" [Ṭabarī].

**Grammar — "indeed it is heavy".** The pronoun: **[Ṭabarī]** holds it refers to the prayer, and rejects the reading that makes it the acceptance of Muḥammad ﷺ, since nothing in the passage mentions that acceptance, and one does not leave the evident sense for a hidden one without evidence. **[Qurṭubī]** sets out the field: it may be the prayer alone, since prayer imprisons the soul as fasting does not — the fasting man gives up one or two appetites and then roams free in speech and movement, while the one praying is restrained in all his limbs; or it may cover both patience and prayer but be expressed with the pronoun of the more prevalent, as 9:34 says of gold and silver "nor spend *it*", and as 62:11 speaks of trade and amusement and "they rushed *to it*"; or the patience is included in the prayer, since patience is of the prayer's content; or the reference is to the worship these two contain, or to the seeking of help itself, or to the Kaʿbah. What all agree on is the meaning of the exception: it is light for those whom God has confirmed.

**Rulings & āthār.** **[Qurṭubī]** treats patience as a topic in its own right. The command is to be patient *upon* obedience and *against* opposition — al-Naḥḥās: one says of a man that he is *ṣābir* against sins; when he restrains himself from them he has been patient upon obedience. And very early in Islam the meaning of patience was summed up: ʿAlī said, as al-Shaʿbī reports, "Patience is to faith as the head is to the body" — and then he raised his voice: "Truly, there is no faith in one who has no patience." **[Ṭabarī]** records the saying and affirms it, with the reasoning: faith is knowledge of the heart, confession of the tongue and action of the limbs, and one who does not hold himself to the actions of his limbs does not deserve the name of faith at all — so patience in the acts the law requires is the head without which the body has no life. Yaḥyā b. al-Yamān's definition: patience is not to wish for a state other than what God has provided, and contentment with what He decrees in this world and the next. And the reward is set beyond measure: "The patient shall be paid their wage without reckoning" (39:10) — while other good deeds have a stated multiple, the wage of the patient is left unnumbered [Qurṭubī]. On the saying of the Prophet ﷺ in al-Bukhārī's report from Abū Mūsā — "There is none more patient over the harm he hears than God: they ascribe a son to Him, yet He heals them and provides for them" — the scholars explain: to describe God as patient is to describe Him as forbearing, and the divine name *al-Ṣabūr* is the intensive form of that forbearance toward those who disobey Him [Qurṭubī]. And on the practice the verse recommends, from Ḥudhayfah: "When the Messenger of God ﷺ was troubled by a matter, he would turn to the prayer" — and ʿAbd Allāh b. ʿAbbās, told of the death of his brother Qutham while he was on a journey, said, "A covering God has concealed, a burden God has spared, and a reward God has brought," then stepped off the road, prayed, and returned to his mount reciting, "Seek help in patience and prayer" [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī] [Jalālayn].

**Belief & reflection.** **[Study Quran]** notes the two senses of *ṣabr* — endurance of hardship and steadfastness in good — and that "this is difficult" can refer to the prayer or to the admonition as a whole: a person may hold to a delayed gratification with patience, but prayer always demands a retreat from the world and a shrinking of the ego, and that is what is easy only to the humble. **[Maʿārif]** had already connected the verse backward: what made the People of the Book hesitate was the love of money and power, and patience cures the first while the prayer cures the second — for prayer is humility in form and content, five times daily, with the renunciation of even lawful things at fixed hours, which is heavy until the heart learns *khushūʿ*, which he renders as restfulness: the mind cannot move in two directions at once, so the Qurʾān gives the heart one object to rest in, and the other thoughts fall away. Put simply, the verse tells the scholar reproached in 2:44 where to go with his burden: not to the crowd he lectures but to patience and to the prayer — held, not merely professed; and the second of the two is only light for those who have already bowed inside.


## 2:46

*"Those who are certain that they will meet their Lord, and that to Him they are returning."*

**Meaning.** The verse describes the *khāshiʿīn* of 2:45 and in doing so explains why the prayer is light for them: they know they are on their way to their Lord, and that nothing they do falls outside the meeting [Ibn Kathīr] [Ṭabarī]. **[Ṭabarī]** draws the consequence: whoever does not believe in a return or a reckoning finds the prayer mere weariness and error, since he hopes for no benefit from it and fears no harm in dropping it; but for one who expects God's abundant reward for it and dreads His punishment for leaving it, the prayer becomes easy. So God commanded the rabbis of the Children of Israel to be of those who establish it with hope — if they are a people certain that they are returning to God and meeting Him at the Resurrection.

**Language — the ẓann problem.** **[Ṭabarī]** opens with the question: how can God describe the humble as *thinking* (*yaẓunnūna*) they will meet Him, when *ẓann* is doubt, and doubt about the meeting with God is disbelief? His answer: the Arabs call certainty *ẓann* and doubt *ẓann* — as they call darkness *sadfah* and light *sadfah*, the one who aids *ṣārikh* and the one who cries for aid *ṣārikh* — the same name for a thing and its opposite. He proves the certainty-sense with Durayd b. al-Ṣimmah's line, "I said to them: *suppose* two thousand mailed men — their best in Persian mail," meaning: be certain of them; and with 18:53, "and the guilty saw the Fire and *thought* they were about to fall into it" — where no uncertainty is possible [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī]. The reports: Abū al-ʿĀliyah — "ẓann here is certainty"; Mujāhid, twice — "every *ẓann* in the Qurʾān is knowledge"; al-Suddī — they are certain; Ibn Zayd — because they have not yet witnessed it, their ẓann was a certainty, not a doubt [Ṭabarī]. **[Qurṭubī]** adds the fine point: ẓann in this sense is used only of what has not come into the senses — no Arab says of a man standing before him, "I think this is a human being"; but of the unseen it may carry the force of knowledge. And he records al-Mahdawī and al-Mawarrdī's attempt to keep ẓann on its literal sense by supposing an ellipsis — they expect to meet Him *as sinners* — and Ibn ʿAṭiyyah's verdict: that is a stretch. Al-Farrāʾ even claimed *ẓann* can mean lying, which the Baṣran grammarians did not recognize [Qurṭubī].

**Language — "meeting their Lord".** **[Qurṭubī]** explains *liqāʾ* here as the reckoning of their Lord — the encounter that every soul has with the account of its deeds. **[Ṭabarī]** takes up the grammar of *mulāqū rabbihim*: the form is that of a noun built from a verb, and the Baṣrans held the *nūn* was dropped for lightness though it was intended, as in "every soul is the taster of death" (3:185) and "We are the senders of the she-camel as a trial" (54:27) — where the sending had not yet happened; the Kufans held that the construct form is proper to the noun-form while the *nūn* belongs to the verbal sense, so the construct is for the wording and the retention of the *nūn* for the meaning. And they differed over "returning": Abū al-ʿĀliyah — they are certain they will return to Him on the Day of Resurrection; others — the return is by death. **[Ṭabarī]** prefers the first, since 2:28 had already said, "then He will give you life, then to Him you shall be returned" — a return after the raising, which is the Day of Resurrection.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** cites the ḥadīth in the Ṣaḥīḥ: on the Day of Resurrection God will say to a servant, "Did I not let you marry, honour you, subject horses and camels to you, and let you rule and be ruled?" — "Yes." — "Did you not *think* you would meet Me?" — "No." — "This day I forget you as you forgot Me." And he cites Ibn ʿAbbās on the *khāshiʿīn*: those who believe in what God has sent down [Ibn Kathīr] [Ṭabarī].

**Belief & reflection.** **[Maʿārif]** devotes this verse's section to the quality the verse crowns — *khushūʿ*, the humbleness of the heart. It is a restfulness and a humility born of the awareness of God's majesty and of one's own smallness beside it; once acquired, it makes obedience easy and even pleasant, and at times shows itself in the bearing of the man who has it, who seems broken of vanity. But he warns against the counterfeit: if a man does not carry real humility and fear of God in his heart, no amount of downcast looks and external modesty will give it to him — and it is not even right to *put on* the signs deliberately. ʿUmar saw a young man sitting with his head bowed and said to him, "Raise your head! Humbleness of heart is in the heart." Ibrāhīm al-Nakhaʿī defined it: it is not wearing rough clothes, eating coarse food and keeping the head low; it is to treat the high and low alike in matters of truth, and to keep the heart free to devote itself to God and to what He has made obligatory. And al-Ḥasan said of ʿUmar himself: he spoke loud enough to be heard, walked swiftly, and struck hard — and he was, for all that, a man of true humbleness. Then **[Maʿārif]** sets out the juristic question of whether *khushūʿ* is a condition of the prayer's validity: al-Ghazālī reports that Muʿādh, Sufyān al-Thawrī and al-Ḥasan held the unmindful prayer invalid, while the four imāms and most jurists held that what is required is the heart's turning to God with the intention at the opening *takbīr* — that whoever fails to attain *khushūʿ* through the rest of the prayer loses its reward but has not abandoned the prayer — since the law does not impose what men cannot sustain, and only a few can hold *khushūʿ* through the whole prayer. His conclusion is the verse's own balance of hope and fear.

**Cross-references.** 2:45; 2:28 ("then to Him you shall be returned"); 3:185; 54:27; 18:53; 20:14 and 7:205 for remembering God in prayer; 9:67 [Ṭabarī] [Ibn Kathīr] [Maʿārif].

**Reflection.** The verse supplies the whole mechanism of the lightened prayer: it is not that the humble find prayer easy because they are idle or have no battles; they find it easy because they are *certain of the meeting*. A man who knows he is going somewhere keeps his appointments; the heavy thing about the five prayers is not their length but the doubt — the half-sense that nothing is actually waiting at the other end of life. Faith in the meeting changes the arithmetic of everything: whatever is done for it is short, and whatever is lost for its sake is small.


## 2:47

*"O Children of Israel, remember My favour which I bestowed upon you, and that I favoured you over the worlds."*

**Meaning.** The address of 2:40 returns in the same words, with one thing added: not only remember the favours, remember the rank — that God preferred them above the peoples of their age [Jalālayn] [Ṭabarī]. **[Ṭabarī]** explains the repetition as deliberate: the verse re-opens the appeal at the point where the long account of the covenant and the warnings has been given, so that the call to remember comes back to them with the honour of their fathers attached.

**Context — what the preference means.** **[Ṭabarī]** holds that the verse names *their* ancestors' favour, and treats the favour shown to fathers as a favour to their sons, since the sons are honoured by the fathers' honour. And he insists the wording is general but the meaning particular: God means the world of their own time, not every people of every age. The reports agree: Qatāda — He favoured them over the world of their day; Abū al-ʿĀliyah — by the kingship, prophethood and Books given them, over the world of that time, "for every age has its world"; Mujāhid, twice — over those among whom they lived; Ibn Zayd — "the world of the people of that time", reading 44:32, "We chose them knowingly over the worlds," and adding that such verses belong to those of them who obeyed and followed the command — for among them were the transformed apes, the most hateful of God's creatures to Him (2:65). And on the same principle, he says, 3:110, "You are the best community brought out for mankind", is for those who obey God, follow His command and avoid what He has forbidden [Ṭabarī].

**The Prophet's ﷺ word on it.** **[Ṭabarī]** records the ḥadīth of Bahz b. Ḥakīm, from his father, from his grandfather — a sound chain through a Companion — that he heard the Messenger of God ﷺ say: "You are seventy nations, of which you are the last" — and in al-Ḥasan's version, "you are their best and most honoured before God." The point Ṭabarī draws is exact: the Prophet's ﷺ statement shows that the Children of Israel were not preferred above the community of Muḥammad ﷺ; the preference of the verse is the preference of their own age and world, as 45:16 and 44:32 carry the same sense. **[Study Quran]** notes the same conclusion among the commentators: since 3:110 calls the community of the Prophet ﷺ the best brought out for mankind, some read the pre-eminence of Israel as confined to the past, and understand it in the light of 5:20 — Mūsā's reminder, "O my people, remember God's blessing upon you, when He appointed prophets among you, made you kings, and gave you what He gave no one else among the worlds." And it notes that *ʿālamīn* — often "the worlds" in the phrase "Lord of the worlds" — can mean peoples or nations, as in 21:71, "the land which We blessed for all peoples"; the more universal sense, it adds, is not incorrect in itself.

**Reflection.** The verse is the hinge of the whole section, and it is worth catching what the hinge is made of. After the covenant-breach, the concealment, the reproach of the scholars and the call to patience and prayer, God does not begin again with a rebuke: He begins again with *remember the favour* — and then names the highest thing He ever gave them. It was a real rank, and the Qurʾān never denies it. What it does is place the rank in time: a people was raised above its world to carry the Book, and that carried with it the duty to recognise the Book's last Messenger. The favour of the fathers is the honour of the sons; but a favour remembered is meant to be honoured, not used as a shield.


## 2:48

*"And guard yourselves against a Day when no soul will repay another in anything, nor will intercession be accepted from it, nor ransom taken from it, nor will they be helped."*

**Meaning.** Having reminded them of the favour, God warns them of the Day when every claim of privilege collapses: no one repays another's debt, no intercessor is heard for them, no ransom is taken, and no ally comes [Ibn Kathīr] [Ṭabarī]. **[Ṭabarī]** sets out what "repay" means: to pay what another owes and stand in his place — a thing men do for a son or father here; but on that Day the merchandise is the good deeds and the bad ones. **[Qurṭubī]** adds the formulation: no soul is held to account for another's sin, and none pushes anything away from another. Al-Suddī: *tajzī* means *tughnī*, to avail. And the Prophet ﷺ described the economy exactly, and the scholars drew from it the sense: no dinar, no dirham — if the wrongdoer has good deeds they are taken from him by measure of the wrong; if not, the wrongs of his victim are loaded onto him.

**Context.** **[Ṭabarī]** reports why the warning was needed: they used to say, "We are the sons of God and His beloved, and the children of prophets, and our fathers will intercede for us before Him." So God told them that no soul repays another anything on the Resurrection, and no one's intercession is accepted for it until every claimant has received his due in full — and that nothing will profit them with Him but repentance from their disbelief. And, he says, what was decreed for that generation is made a lesson for all who tread the same road, so that no one may presume upon God's mercy. **[Maʿārif]** explains the same point for the reader of today: the verse removes the illusion of vicarious religious credit — the suggestion that a man short in prayer and fasting might have someone else's worship transferred to his account. That transaction does not exist on that Day. **[Ibn Kathīr]** gathers the cross-references: 35:18, "no bearer of burdens bears another's burden"; 80:37, "every man that Day has enough to occupy him"; and 31:33, "no father can avail his son, nor a son avail his father" — a terror, since the bonds of blood are the last thing men expect to fail.

**Áthār & ḥadīth.** **[Ṭabarī]** records through three chains from Abū Hurayrah the report the scholars called ṣaḥīḥ — "If a man has a wrong against his brother — in honour, or wealth, or standing — let him seek release from it today, before there is neither dinar nor dirham: if he has good deeds, they are taken from him by the measure of the wrong; and if he has no good deeds, the wrongs of his companion are loaded on him." He gives a fourth from ʿUthmān b. ʿAffān that on that Day even the hornless sheep will take its requital from the horned — *(weak)* the chain runs through al-Ḥajjāj b. Nuṣayr, of whom the critics spoke, though Ibn Kathīr's ground meaning is established elsewhere. And from Ibn ʿAbbās: "Let none of you die with a debt on him, for there is no dinar or dirham there; there they divide good deeds and evil deeds" — the Prophet ﷺ motioned right and left.

**Language.** *Al-ʿadl* with the fatḥah is ransom, the equivalent given in exchange, from the same root as *iʿtidāl*, balance: what a man offers as his equal [Ṭabarī] [Study Quran]. **[Study Quran]** traces its range — it can mean an earth full of gold (3:91), twice all that is on the earth (5:36; 13:18), even one's own children (70:11). *Shafāʿah* is from *shafʿ*, the pair: the intercessor makes the needy man *two* to the petition instead of the *one* he was, joining his standing to the other's need; the *shufʿah* — the right of pre-emption in a neighbour's property — comes from the same joining of one's ownership to another's [Qurṭubī] [Ṭabarī]. **[Qurṭubī]** notes the grammar: some read *tajzī* and some *tujziʾ* with the hamzah, and whether *jazā* and *ajzāʾ* are one verb or two — *jazā* meaning to pay and requite, *ajzāʾ* to suffice — the sense in this verse comes to the same.

**Rulings — intercession and its limits.** **[Qurṭubī]** takes up the great question here. The people of truth hold that intercession is real; the Muʿtazilah denied it altogether, and made the believing sinners who enter the Fire abide there forever. Against them: the reports are mass-transmitted in meaning that the disobedient believers among the communities of the prophets are reached by the intercession of angels, prophets, martyrs and the righteous; and the Salaf of every age accepted those reports without one voice of objection, which is decisive. To their proof texts — "the wrongdoers shall have no loyal friend nor an intercessor who is heard" (40:18), "whoever does evil shall be requited for it" (4:123), this verse itself — the answer is that the general wording is not absolute: the passages treat the disbelievers, as 74:48 makes plain, "the intercession of the intercessors shall not profit them," while 21:28 says the angels "intercede only for those He is pleased with," and 34:23 limits it by God's permission, and 19:87, "they shall not possess intercession except he who has taken a covenant with the Most Merciful." And the strongest word of all: "My intercession is for the people of the grave sins of my community," and, "Every prophet has been given a supplication, and I have kept mine back as intercession for my community; it will reach, God willing, those of them who do not associate anything with God." **[Ṭabarī]** reaches the same conclusion: the prohibition of intercession here concerns one who dies in his disbelief unrepentant. **[Maʿārif]** frames the theological point: the Muʿtazilah and later groups denied all intercession for Muslims on the strength of this verse, but the negation is specific to the unbeliever — the Qurʾān affirms intercession elsewhere (53:26; 34:23; 20:109) and denies it for those without faith (21:28). **[Study Quran]** sets out the wider landscape: intercession and ransom were ordinary transactions of tribal society, so the denial of them in the Hereafter is the denial of earthly status as currency; yet 20:109 and 39:44 — "unto God belongs intercession altogether" — and 2:255c leave room, and many read the denials as aimed at the bargaining, status-driven intercession of this world, not at the prayer of the righteous which is itself a gift of God's mercy.

**Belief & reflection.** The verse names four doors and closes each: kinship, intercession, wealth, and help. Wealth is *ʿadl* — the ransom a rich man pays; help is the strong friend's arm. What remains is what the next verses offer: mercy received on terms, and a turning back. And **[Study Quran]**'s observation that *ittaqā* here also means to be on guard keeps the verse from becoming only a report about a distant day: it is an instruction to keep watch now, because the four doors it shuts are the four things men lean on while telling themselves there will be time.


## 2:49

*"And when We delivered you from the house of Pharaoh, who were visiting you with evil torment, slaughtering your sons and sparing your women — and in that was a great trial from your Lord."*

**Meaning.** The reminder now becomes a history: the mercy of God in taking the Children of Israel out of Pharaoh's grip [Saʿdī] [Jalālayn]. **[Saʿdī]** describes the condition before the deliverance: they were killing your sons out of fear that you would grow in number, and keeping your women alive — so the people stood between a corpse and a man humiliated with hard labour, and the women's lives were spared as a favour done *over* them, which is the extremity of humiliation. Then God showed them favour with complete deliverance and the drowning of their enemy while they watched, so that their eyes might be comforted. *And in that was a great trial from your Lord* — trial here, he says, means kindness: it obliges you to thank Him and stand by His commands.

**Context.** The address is to the living while the events befell their forefathers: *idh* is attached to "remember My favour", and the deliverance of the fathers is reckoned to the sons because it is the kind of blessing whose benefit descends — as 69:11 says, "when the water overflowed, We carried you in the ship", meaning your fathers [Qurṭubī] [Jalālayn]. **[Ṭabarī]** justifies the usage with a line of poetry: al-Akhṭal reviles Jarīr's tribe with a battle at which Jarīr was not even born — "al-Hudhayl rose against you and reached you at Irāb" — because it was the deed of his people; so God attributes to the present generation what He did to their ancestors.
*(Isrāʾīliyyāt)* The reports on why the slaughter began: **[Ibn Kathīr]** — Pharaoh saw in a dream a fire coming out of Bayt al-Maqdis and entering the houses of the Egyptians while passing over the Israelites, meaning his kingdom would be toppled by a man of the Children of Israel; others say his courtiers told him the Israelites were expecting a man to arise among them and establish a state. **[Jalālayn]** — some of their priests told him, "a child born among the Israelites will bring about the end of your rule." **[Ṭabarī]** gives Ibn ʿAbbās's fuller account: Pharaoh and his court recalled God's promise to Abraham of prophets and kings in his progeny, and agreed to send men with knives through the land, killing every male newborn; when they saw the elders dying in their time and the infants being killed, they feared the Israelites would be exhausted as a workforce — so they killed the male children one year and left them the next. Hārūn was born in the year of mercy; Mūsā the year after. And through ʿIkrimah from Ibn ʿAbbās: the soothsayers told Pharaoh that a child would be born that year who would take his kingdom, so he placed a hundred men over every thousand women. **[Study Quran]** observes that the Qurʾān itself gives no reason for the killing and, noting that Pharaoh repeats the same threat when Mūsā is grown (7:127; 40:25), reads it as collective punishment and control rather than the fulfilment of a predicted fate — though it records that Ṭabarī, al-Rāzī and Ibn Kathīr pass on the forecast of soothsayers or astrologers. The killer detail **[Ṭabarī]** preserves from Ibn Isḥāq is the machinery of it: Pharaoh made the Israelites servants and slaves, sorting them into those who built, those who ploughed, those who sowed for him, and those who served — and whoever among them was in none of these was charged the *jizyah*; al-Suddī says he put them to filthy work.

**Language.** *Āl* is *ahl*, "people": the *hāʾ* was exchanged for an *alif* — the way *māh* becomes *māʾ* — and it returns to the *hāʾ* in the diminutive, *uhayl* [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī]. *Āl Firʿawn* are his people, his followers, his religion — not his blood only, and **[Qurṭubī]** builds the point: the *āl* of the Prophet ﷺ in this usage are those upon his religion in his time and every age, kin or not; Abū Lahab and Abū Jahal are not of his *āl* or his *ahl* however close the kinship — as an-Nūḥ's son was told, "he is not of your family" (11:46). And the proof from 40:46, "Enter, house of Pharaoh, the severest punishment" — *āli dīnihi*, since the man had no son, daughter, father, uncle or brother at all. *Firʿawn*: **[Ṭabarī]** and **[Ibn Kathīr]** — it was the title of the Amalekite kings of Egypt, like Qayṣar for Rome, Kisrā for Persia, Najāshī for Abyssinia, Tubbaʿ for Yemen; **[Qurṭubī]** adds the name of Mūsā's Pharaoh: al-Walīd b. Muṣʿab b. al-Rayyān, of the line of ʿImlīq, kunya Abū Murrah; in the People of the Book's account, Qābūs. And every tyrant is a *Firʿawn*. *Yasūmūnakum*: Abū ʿUbaydah — "they humiliate you" or "they impose on you", from *sāma ḫuṭṭata kh asf*, as ʿAmr b. Kulthūm says, "when the king imposes humiliation on a people"; others — the *sāma* is what is continuous, as the freely grazing flock continues to graze [Qurṭubī] [Ibn Kathīr]. *Sawʾ al-ʿadhāb*: **[Ṭabarī]** refines it, against the common gloss "the worst": if it meant worst it would have been *aswaʾ*; it means the punishment that *grieves* them — which is named exactly in the rest of the verse.

**Rulings.** **[Ṭabarī]** draws a legal conclusion from the wording — that God attributes the killing to the *house* of Pharaoh rather than to Pharaoh himself, though they did it by his order and his power — because the one who personally carries out the killing or the torture is the one to whom the deed belongs. Whoever himself kills a soul unjustly is the slayer against whom retaliation is due, even if he did it under the compulsion of another's command, whether that commander be an authority, a marauder or a usurper. **[Qurṭubī]**, in his treatment of *āl*, gives the doctrinal ruling on the family of the Prophet ﷺ that follows from the same usage, including the ḥadīth from ʿAmr b. al-ʿĀṣ in Muslim — heard openly, not in secret — that the family of Abū so-and-so are not his allies: "my ally is God and the righteous believers." And he records the word of Abū Ḥumayd al-Sāʿidī, taught by the Prophet ﷺ for the blessing of prayer: "O God, bless Muḥammad and his wives and his offspring, as You blessed the family of Ibrāhīm."

**Belief & reflection.** The verse closes on *balāʾ* — a word that **[Study Quran]** notes is lexically a testing and finding out, here usually tied to the sufferings but understood by some (Ṭabarī among them) as favour, since 2:47 used the same sense of blessing; and the Qurʾān uses one word because the two faces are one thing: "We try you with evil and with good as a trial" (21:35), and "We tried them with good things and with evil things" (7:168) [Study Quran] [Ibn Kathīr]. What the verse is asking of its hearers is that both kinds of *balāʾ* should produce the same result — the man drowning in hard labour and the man walking out of the sea are being asked the same question. **[Saʿdī]** puts the obligation at the end, and it stands for the whole passage: mercy remembered is meant to become gratitude, and gratitude means standing by the commands of the One who gave the mercy.


## 2:50

*"And when We parted the sea for you, and delivered you, and drowned the house of Pharaoh while you looked on."*

**Meaning.** The second panel of the same deliverance: the water opened, Israel crossed, and the army behind them went under — with the eye open the whole time [Jalālayn] [Saʿdī]. **[Saʿdī]** makes the observation at the end of the previous verse do its work here: the deliverance was completed and the enemy drowned *while they watched*, so that their eyes might be comforted — that is, the mercy was not merely rescue but vindication seen.

**Language.** *Faraqnā* is to separate and divide, as one parts the hair; from the same root come *al-Furqān*, because it separates truth from falsehood, "the day of *al-furqān*" (8:41) at Badr, and "a Qurʾān which We have *faraqnāhu*" (17:106) — We set it out and established it. Al-Zuhrī read *farraqnā* with a doubled *rāʾ*: We made it into parts. And the *bāʾ* in *bikum*: "for you", or, keeping its own force, "We parted the sea *by means of* you" — the parting happened by their entering it, so the sea stood as two walls with them between, which is borne out by "and it split" (26:63) [Qurṭubī] [Study Quran]. **[Ṭabarī]** rejects the Baṣran gloss that it means "We separated between you and the water", since God said He parted the sea *by* the people, not between them and it — and what that parting was, he says, is what the reports describe: twelve tribes, twelve roads, a tribe to each path. *Al-bahr* is the salt sea, named for its vastness; a horse is *baḥr* when its running is wide; and the Prophet ﷺ said of Mandūb, Abū Ṭalḥah's horse, "we found him a sea" [Qurṭubī].

**Context — how it happened.** From Ibn Isḥāq by way of Muḥammad b. Kaʿb al-Quraẓī and ʿAbd Allāh b. Shaddād: Pharaoh set out after Mūsā with seventy thousand dark horses besides the piebalds; Mūsā reached the sea with nowhere to turn, and when the two hosts saw one another the companions of Mūsā cried, "We are surely overtaken," and Mūsā said, "No — my Lord is with me; He will guide me" (26:61–62), meaning to deliverance: "He has promised me, and His promise is not broken." The sea had been told: when Mūsā strikes you, split — and it spent the night churning, out of fear of God and waiting for His command; then Mūsā struck it with the staff in which God had placed His authority, and "each part was like a great mountain" — a hill standing up out of the ground [Ṭabarī]. **[Ṭabarī]** also gives, without isnād to a Companion, the account of the crossing's end: when Israel had all entered and none remained, Pharaoh came to the sea's edge on his horse, which shied from entering; Jibrīl appeared before it on a mare in season, and the stallion followed her in, and the army followed Pharaoh; Jibrīl went out ahead of them, Mīkāʾīl drove them from behind saying, "Catch up with your master," and when the last of Israel was across and the last of Egypt was in, the sea closed over them; then Pharaoh, seeing God's power and knowing his own humiliation, cried, "I believe that there is no god but the One the Children of Israel believe in, and I am of those who submit" (10:90) [Ṭabarī].

**Stories & occasions.** *(Isrāʾīliyyāt)* The fullest narrative in the corpus is ʿAbd Allāh b. Masʿūd's, through ʿAmr b. Maymūn: when Mūsā left with Israel, Pharaoh said, "Let none follow them until the rooster crows" — and no rooster crowed that night; he called for a sheep to be slaughtered and said, "I shall not be done with its liver until six hundred thousand Copts have gathered to me," and he was not done with it before six hundred thousand had gathered. At the sea, Yūshaʿ b. Nūn asked Mūsā, "Where has your Lord commanded you?" — "In front of you," pointing to the sea. Yūshaʿ drove his horse in until it was at the depth, was carried off, and came back; he did that three times, saying, "By God, you have not lied and you have not been lied to." Then God revealed, "Strike the sea with your staff", and the sea, which had spent the night trembling, not knowing from which side it would be struck, opened into twelve paths, one for each tribe, each like a great mountain; the tribes complained they could not see one another, so Mūsā struck the water-walls with his staff and there were windows in them through which they could see each other, until they came out. Then Pharaoh entered with his host, on a long-tailed black stallion that feared to plunge, and Jibrīl again drew it in with a mare; the last of the people of Mūsā cleared the far side, the last of the people of Pharaoh entered, and the sea closed [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī]. **[Saʿdī]** keeps the whole narration of the passage in one sweep — the warning that they would be followed, the drowning of the enemy before their eyes — and includes the report that 12,000 of Israel, or by another count 600,000 fighting men, faced Pharaoh's 1,100,000 horses [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī].

**Ḥadīth & āthār — ʿĀshūrāʾ.** Neither the Qurʾān nor these verses name the day; the day is fixed in the Sunnah. **Ṭabarī** [sic: Muslim], al-Bukhārī and others report from Ibn ʿAbbās: the Prophet ﷺ came to Madīnah and found the Jews fasting ʿĀshūrāʾ; he asked what it was, and they said, "A great day on which God delivered Mūsā and his people and drowned Pharaoh and his people; Mūsā fasted it in thanks." The Prophet ﷺ said, "We have more right to Mūsā than you" — and fasted it and commanded its fasting [Qurṭubī] [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Qurṭubī]** raises the objection to reading this as mere imitation: ʿĀʾishah reported that ʿĀshūrāʾ was a day Quraysh fasted in the Age of Ignorance and the Prophet ﷺ fasted it then; when he came to Madīnah he fasted it and commanded it, and when Ramaḍān was prescribed the obligation shifted and ʿĀshūrāʾ was left as one may choose. The virtue of it remains in the reports: it expiates the year before it.

**Belief & rulings.** **[Maʿārif]** draws the doctrinal point the verse is often used to defend: the splitting of the sea proves that miracles (*muʿjizāt*) do occur at the hands of prophets — the point directed at those of Westernized education among Muslims who have tried to deny them. And **[Study Quran]** notes the scene is retold in the Sūrah of Provisions of Mercy: drowning as a punishment appears again in the story of Nūḥ, in 17:69 and 36:43, and most authorities identify this unnamed sea as the Red Sea.

**Reflection.** The two halves of the verse are the two ends of one mercy: God saved by opening a road where none existed, and destroyed by closing the one that had just been used. The detail that matters most is at the end — "while you looked on." God did not merely take them out of Egypt; He let them watch the end of the thing that had owned them. For a people leaving generations of slavery, seeing is part of the cure, and the same scene is the reason the fast of ʿĀshūrāʾ has stayed alive among people who never saw it.


## 2:51

*"And when We appointed for Mūsā forty nights — then you took the calf in his absence, and you were wrongdoers."*

**Meaning.** The favour of the Book is set beside the infidelity of the receivers: while Mūsā was on the mountain for the appointment that would bring them the Torah, they took a calf as a god — and God names them by what they were: *wrongdoers* [Jalālayn] [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Ṭabarī]** explains the point of the reminder: God is telling the Jews of the Prophet's day about the deed of their fathers — their denial of their prophets in the midst of favours that came one after another — so that they might recognise themselves as walking the same road, and be warned that what fell on the first deniers may fall on them.

**Language — the reading.** *Wāʿadnā* or *waʿadnā*: **[Ṭabarī]** sets out the two positions. Those who read *wāʿadnā* argued that every *mutual* appointment for a meeting is an appointment from both sides, each of them promising the other the encounter; those who read *waʿadnā* argued that *muwāʿadah* belongs to human equals, whereas God is alone in promise and threat, and the Qurʾān everywhere says "God promised you" (14:22), "God has promised those who believe" (24:55), "when God promised you one of the two parties" (8:7). **[Ṭabarī]**'s own verdict: both readings came down from the community and neither invalidates the other. The apparent addition in one is not a difference in meaning, because when God promised Mūsā the meeting at the mountain, Mūsā's acceptance of it was itself a promise — God did not appoint the mountain except with Mūsā's willing response. **[Qurṭubī]** records the same debate with the readers' names — Abū ʿAmr and Abū Ḥātim preferred *waʿadnā*, al-Naḥḥās called *wāʿadnā* the better, and it was the reading of Mujāhid, al-Aʿraj, Ibn Kathīr, Nāfiʿ, Ḥamzah, al-Kisāʾī and al-Aʿmash; and al-Zajjāj's reasoning: Mūsā's obedience and acceptance stand in the place of a promise from his side. And *Mūsā*: in Coptic, *mū* is water and *shā* is tree — he was found in the ark at a place of water and trees by Pharaoh's house, so he was named for the place where he was found; it is a foreign name, and does not take the genitive [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī]. His lineage, per Ibn Isḥāq: Mūsā b. ʿImrān b. Yaṣhar b. Qāhith b. Lāwī b. Yaʿqūb — Israel — b. Isḥāq b. Ibrāhīm [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī]. *Ittakhadhtum* is *iftāʿaltum* from *akhdh*, the second hamzah eased away for the collision of two hamzahs; *al-ʿijl* is the calf — and **[Qurṭubī]** notes a suggested reason for the name: they named it so from the haste (*ʿajalah*) with which they took to its worship.

**Context — the forty nights.** Abū al-ʿĀliyyah: Dhū al-Qaʿdah and ten from Dhū al-Ḥijjah; Mūsā left his people, appointed Hārūn over them, stayed on the mountain forty nights, and the Torah was sent down to him in the Tablets — which were of hail-stone, per the report [Ṭabarī]. Ibn Isḥāq: God promised thirty nights and completed them with ten, and Mūsā went out to his Lord eagerly, asking that his brother succeed him among the people and not follow the way of the corrupters [Ṭabarī]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** joins it to 7:142, "We appointed for Mūsā thirty nights and completed them with ten." **[Qurṭubī]** gives the sequence that produced the calf: Mūsā went out to the mountain with seventy of the best of Israel, and the appointment was forty nights; the people counted twenty days and twenty nights and said, "He has broken his promise to us," so they took the calf, with al-Sāmirī telling them, "This is your god and the god of Mūsā" (20:88) — and they settled into his word, though Hārūn forbade them and said, "O my people, you have only been tried by it; your Lord is the Compassionate; so follow me and obey my command" (20:90). They answered, "We will not cease to be its devotees until Mūsā returns to us."

**Rulings — why nights, not days.** **[Qurṭubī]** raises the question and gives the answer: the night precedes the day in rank, and by it dates are reckoned — the nights open the months and the days follow. And he records al-Naqqāsh's inference, that the verse points to the joining of fasts, since had God mentioned the days one might have thought Mūsā broke his fast at night; specifying the *nights* carries the force that he continued forty days with their nights. From this, he says, the Sufis took their evidence for continuous fasting, and held the best of it forty days.

**Stories & occasions.** *(Isrāʾīliyyāt)* The narrative of how al-Sāmirī obtained the dust is told at length by Ibn ʿAbbās through ʿIkrimah: he recognised Jibrīl when he saw him at the sea, driving Pharaoh's horse into the water, and took a handful from beneath the hoof of the horse; and Ibn Masʿūd is reported to have read 20:96 as *"a handful from the trace of the horse of the messenger."* Al-Sāmirī's mother had left him in a cave as an infant, and Jibrīl came and fed him from his fingers — milk from one, honey from another, butter from another — until he grew [Ṭabarī]. **[Maʿārif]** tells the outcome: the calf, made of gold or silver and animated by the dust, acquired life, and the ignorant among them worshipped it. **[Qurṭubī]** also records the detail that Hārūn was followed in refusing it by only twelve thousand, while the rest — more than two million, by that report — fell into its worship. These narratives belong to the Israelite reports the commentators transmit; the Qurʾān's own account is the summary at 7:148–155 and 20:83–98, and **[Maʿārif]** keeps that distance: what the verse itself says is that a man named al-Sāmirī fashioned the figure and the people who had been given so much fell to worshipping it.

**Reflection.** The word the verse ends on is the one that explains the rest: *wrongdoers* — **[Maʿārif]** defines the term exactly, that injustice lies in putting a thing where it does not belong, and that is what idolatry is at the root. The scene has a hard symmetry worth noticing: a people who had just been carried through the sea, whose God had drowned an empire for them, made a god out of a metal calf in the weeks while their prophet was away receiving their Book. The distance between a delivered people and a calf is not as long as it looks; it is the length of a wait that grew inconvenient.


## 2:52

*"Then We pardoned you after that, so that you might give thanks."*

**Meaning.** The sentence runs directly into the next: the pardon of 2:52 came after the repentance described in 2:54 — "and when We said, 'Strike him with a part of it'" — and here the pardoning is stated plainly, followed by its aim: gratitude [Jalālayn] [Maʿārif]. **[Qurṭubī]** presses the grammar of the aim: *laʿalla* expresses expectation, and it is not that God was in doubt — what it means is that when people receive a pardon, onlookers may expect them to feel grateful.

**Language — ʿafw and shukr.** *Al-ʿafw* is God's passing over His creatures; **[Qurṭubī]** distinguishes it from *maghfirah*: pardon may come before punishment or after it, whereas forgiveness is never accompanied by punishment at all. Whoever deserved a punishment and had it left to him has been pardoned: so *ʿafw* is the erasure of the sin, from the wind that *ʿafā* the trace — blew it away. And the same root carries the opposite sense, increase, as in 7:95, "until they *ʿafaw*" (multiplied) — it is among the words that are their own opposites. *Shukr* in the language is a showing forth: a beast is *shakūr* when it displays fat beyond the feed it is given; the reality of it is praise of a benefactor for a good he has done you [Qurṭubī]. Al-Jawharī: "I thank him" and "I thank *to* him" — the latter more eloquent; *shukrān* is the opposite of *kufrān*. And the ḥadīth of Tirmidhī and Abū Dāwūd from Abū Hurayrah: "He who does not thank people does not thank God." Al-Khaṭṭābī explains it two ways: one whose nature is to deny the kindness of people and leave their good unacknowledged is the sort whose habit is to deny God's favour likewise; or, God will not accept a servant's thanks for His goodness while he refuses to thank the goodness of other men, since the two matters are joined.

**Belief — what gratitude is.** **[Qurṭubī]** collects the sayings of the masters. Sahl b. ʿAbd Allāh: gratitude is striving in obedience and avoiding disobedience, in secret and in public. Some: gratitude is the acknowledgment of one's shortcoming in gratitude itself — which is why God said, "Work, family of Dāwūd, in gratitude" (34:13), and Dāwūd asked, "How can I thank You, my Lord, when the gratitude is itself a favour from You?" and God answered, "Now you have known Me and thanked Me." He asked to be shown the most hidden of God's blessings on him, and God said, "Dāwūd — breathe": and Dāwūd breathed, and God said, "Who can count this blessing of nights and days?" And Mūsā said, "How can I thank You, when the smallest of Your blessings on my hand cannot be repaid by my whole life of work?" and God revealed, "Mūsā, now you have thanked Me." Al-Junayd: the reality of gratitude is the inability to give thanks — and he tells that at seven years old, sitting before al-Sarī al-Saqaṭī while the company spoke of gratitude, he was asked what gratitude is and said, "That God not be disobeyed by means of His blessings," and al-Sarī said to him, "I fear your share of God will be your tongue." Al-Junayd said he never stopped weeping at those words. Shiblī: gratitude is humility, guarding one's good deeds, opposing appetite, giving obedience, and watching the Compeller of earth and heaven. And Dhū al-Nūn al-Miṣrī: gratitude toward those above you is obedience, toward your equals requital, and toward those below you kindness and generosity.
