And "after that" — after the proof: the taking of the covenant and the raising of the mountain [Qurṭubī]. In the grammar of *lawlā faḍlu Allāh*, Sibawayh holds *faḍl* is raised as the subject of an implied predicate that may not be expressed, the Arabs having dispensed with it [Qurṭubī].

**Belief — two kinds of mercy.** **[Maʿārif]** draws the theological distinction the verse implies. God's mercy is of two kinds: a general mercy that reaches believer and disbeliever alike and shows itself as worldly well-being and prosperity, and a special mercy belonging to believers alone, appearing in the Hereafter as salvation and nearness to God; and he notes that the last phrase is addressed to the Jews who were the Prophet's contemporaries, since faith in him was itself part of the covenant, so that they too stand among those who broke it — the verse asks them to recognize that it is by God's mercy alone that the catastrophic punishments which used to descend on earlier traitors have not come down on them in this world. Some commentators, he says, on the strength of authentic hadiths that the sending of the Prophet ﷺ holds back such general destruction, identified this particular favour with the sending of Muḥammad ﷺ itself. **[Ṭabarī]** similarly reads *faḍl* as the favour of repentance granted after they broke the covenant, and mercy as what carried them through to obedience; and he states the principle for the whole passage: the address is to the People of the Book living among the Prophet's emigrants, but the report is about their forebears, put in the form of address to them — as one Arab tribe boasts or reproaches another with the deeds of its ancestors and attributes past deeds to the present.

**Reflection.** Two verses, and a people's whole history in miniature: a covenant taken under a lifted mountain, then backs turned. What makes the close of the verse so heavy is that the mercy is named almost reluctantly — *were it not for* — as though the reader is meant to feel the weight that would have fallen if it had not been there. **[Maʿārif]**'s distinction between mercies is worth keeping for everything that follows in the Sūrah: the general mercy that lets a rebellious people eat and trade and live on is not approval; it is time. The special mercy is another thing entirely, and it is asked for, not presumed.


## 2:65

*"And you have indeed known those among you who transgressed in the matter of the Sabbath, and We said to them: 'Be apes, despised.'"*

**Meaning.** From the broken covenant the Sūrah moves to a case that proves what breaking it looks like in practice — a town that turned a day of rest into a trap [Jalālayn] [Saʿdī]. **[Jalālayn]** identifies them as the inhabitants of Aylah, who fished on the Sabbath when it had been forbidden them, and were told to become apes, rejected — and became so, dying three days later. **[Saʿdī]** points the hearer back to the fuller account in 7:163, where the town by the sea is asked about, the fish coming to them openly on their Sabbath and not coming on the day they did not observe it, as a trial for their rebellion: that great sin required God's anger, and He made them *apes, despised* — abject and humiliated.

**Context.** The story is Sūrah al-Aʿrāf's in detail and this verse's in summary. **[Ibn Kathīr]** describes the transgression: they used deceitful means to avoid honouring the Sabbath — setting nets, ropes and artificial pools before the Sabbath, so that when the fish came in abundance on Saturday as usual they were caught and held through the day, and then collected after the Sabbath ended; their deeds looked lawful on the surface but were in reality wicked, which is why the punishment fitted the crime — they were changed into the animals closest to human form. **[Maʿārif]** supplies the social detail from al-Qurṭubī: the Israelites had first invented clever pretences for catching fish on the Sabbath and gradually began doing it openly; a division grew between the transgressors and those scholars and pious men who tried to dissuade them; when the sinners paid no heed, the pious broke away and lived in a separate part of the town. One day no sound came from the other quarter; they went to look, and found every transgressor changed into an ape — Qatāda adding that the young had become apes and the old swine; the apes recognized their relatives, approached them weeping out of remorse and seeking their help, and then all died after three days.

**Stories & occasions — what they actually did.** **[Maʿārif]** spells out the ruse: on Saturday they would tie one end of a cord to a fish's tail and the other to something on the shore, leaving the fish in the water, and on Sunday they would take it out and eat it. So the offence was not an open breach of the letter but the use of tricks that necessarily cancelled the command — and, worse, mocked it. That, he says, is why they were counted disobedient and rebellious and visited with so dire a punishment.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Qurṭubī]** records from al-Nasāʾī (and al-Tirmidhī, who graded it *ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ*) the report of Ṣafwān b. ʿAssāl: a Jew said to his companion, "Let us go to this prophet" — "Do not say *prophet*, lest he hear you; he has four eyes." They came and asked him about nine clear signs, and he listed them: do not associate anything with God; do not steal; do not commit adultery; do not kill the life God has forbidden except by right; do not walk with a slanderer to a ruler; do not practise sorcery; do not consume usury; do not slander a chaste woman; do not turn your back on the day of battle — "and upon you especially, O Jews, that you do not transgress the Sabbath." They kissed his hands and his feet and said, "We bear witness that you are a prophet." He asked what kept them from following him; they said that Dāwūd had prayed that prophethood never leave his descendants, and they feared that if they followed him the Jews would kill them. And the reports agree that the punishment distinguished among them: those who prohibited the sin openly and withdrew from it were saved, while the rest perished — **[Qurṭubī]** follows al-Aʿrāf's account in holding that the town divided into three parties, the transgressors, those who forbade them, and those who kept silent, and that this is sounder than the view that there were only two.

**Belief — did the transformed leave offspring?** The scholars differed. al-Zajjāj and al-Qāḍī Abū Bakr b. al-ʿArabī held it possible that the apes and swine alive now descend from them; the majority held that a metamorphosed people does not breed — apes and swine existed before this event, and those God transformed perished, no transformed creature ever living beyond three days, neither eating nor drinking nor begetting — Ibn ʿAbbās said it, and Ibn ʿAṭiyyah said it is established from the Prophet ﷺ [Qurṭubī] [Ibn Kathīr] [Maʿārif]. **[Qurṭubī]** sides with the majority and rejects the proofs Ibn ʿArabī adduced for the other view: the ḥadīth in Muslim from Abū Hurayrah that a tribe of the Children of Israel was lost and he supposes it to be the mouse — "it does not drink the milk of camels, but drinks the milk of sheep"; the ḍabb ḥadīth in Muslim, "I do not know, perhaps it is from the generations that were metamorphosed"; and ʿAmr b. Maymūn's account of stoning a monkey in the age of ignorance. His verdict: there is no proof in any of it. The general lesson, however, is Ibn Kathīr's: the punishment of that village was made an example for those around it in its own time and a reminder preserved for those who came after — which is why the Prophet ﷺ in the ḥadīth of Abū Hurayrah warned, "Do not commit what the Jews committed, making lawful what God has forbidden by the meanest of tricks."

**Rulings — stratagems and the law.** **[Maʿārif]** uses the verse for a delicate juristic point: the charge that the master jurists invented *ḥiyal* to help the powerful circumvent the Sharīʿah is a calumny resting on a confusion of meanings — in its technical legal sense *ḥilah* is a device for conforming to the law in a case of necessity or human weakness, not for by-passing it. He gives the Prophet's own example: bartering a measure of good dates for two of bad is a form of usury, so the Prophet ﷺ taught the device of selling the bad dates for cash and buying the good with the cash; the intention there is to conform, and the law is neither negated nor mocked. The Jews of the verse did the opposite: their ruse negated the command and made a mockery of the day. The distinction between a device that preserves the law and a device that mocks it is the verse's own line. **[Ibn Kathīr]** gives the ḥadīth that states it in the Prophet's words, reported by Ibn Baṭṭah with a good chain from Abū Hurayrah: "Do not commit what the Jews committed, making lawful what God has forbidden by the meanest of tricks."

**Belief — was the change real?** The commentators treat the metamorphosis as a literal act of God. Ibn ʿAbbās, in al-ʿAwfī's report, said God changed their bodies into apes and swine — the young to apes, the old to swine; Qatāda described them as howling monkeys with tails, after having been men and women [Ibn Kathīr]. As for the apes and swine alive today, the ḥadīth answers the question: Muslim records from Ibn Masʿūd that people asked the Prophet ﷺ whether the apes and swine were descendants of the metamorphosed Jews, and he told them that apes and swine existed in the world before that, and that when God sends this particular punishment on a people, their line ends with it and they do not breed [Maʿārif]. On the same principle, Ibn ʿAbbās said those who violated the Sabbath were turned into monkeys and then perished without offspring, no transformed person living on the earth more than three days, neither eating nor drinking nor begetting [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Maʿārif]** adds a firm note against those who read the verse as a purely moral transformation — that they merely took on the traits of apes and swine — pointing out that they had already displayed those traits before the punishment, so that such a reading empties the verse of its meaning as a punishment for them and a warning for others; and he observes that God's power to change a form is no harder to accept than the descent of man from apes, which the same people swallow without evidence. **[Study Quran]** notes al-Qurṭubī's comparison to 17:50, "Be stone or iron," where the command is rhetorical — as if to say, "Be apes, as you have chosen to be" — and Mujāhid's view that their hearts were transformed though they did not become apes, comparing them to the ass carrying books (62:5); it also collects the Qurʾānic passages where human beings are likened to animals, and the aḥādīth about people being resurrected in the forms their inner natures had taken.

**Language.** *Al-sabt* is rest and stillness, with ease; a sleeping man is *masbūt* for the stillness of his body, and "We made your sleep a *subāt*" (78:9) is rest for the bodies. It is said the day was named for this because God finished the creation on the day before it [Ṭabarī]. *Khāsiʾīn* is from *khasāʾa*: the outcast, driven away — as a dog is driven off with *ikhsaʾ*; the rhyme has it, "like the dog — say to it *ikhsaʾ* and it slinks away" — meaning, if you drive it off it goes off humbled and small. So the verse's *khāsiʾīn* is "driven away from good, abject, humbled": Mujāhid and Qatāda said *ṣāghirīn*, humbled; al-Rabīʿ, "lowly and humbled"; Ibn ʿAbbās, "humiliated" [Ṭabarī].

**Reflection.** This is the first of the Sūrah's metamorphosis stories, and its edge is aimed less at the ancient village than at the reader: they did not break the Sabbath, they *kept* it — with a cord tied to a fish's tail. A law can be honoured in letter and murdered in intent, and the verse calls that transgression. Which is why **[Maʿārif]**'s defence of *ḥiyal* matters as much as his condemnation of them: the question is never whether one may be clever with the law, but whether the cleverness is in service of obedience or in mockery of it. Their fault was not intelligence; it was contempt wearing the clothes of compliance.


## 2:66

*"So We made it an exemplary punishment for what was before it and what was after it, and an admonition for the reverent."*

**Meaning.** The punishment of the Sabbath-breakers was not left as a private event in one village's history; God made it a public lesson [Ibn Kathīr] [Saʿdī] [Jalālayn]. **[Saʿdī]** states the twofold purpose: as a deterrent for the communities present at the event and those who came after, and as a proof of God's justice against His servants, so that people would hold back from disobedience — with the sober qualification that such warnings profit no one but the reverent. **[Jalālayn]** notes that the reverent are singled out here precisely because they, unlike others, are the ones who benefit from the lesson.

**Language.** *Nakāl* is an infinitive from *nakkala bi-fulān*: it is punishment, as ʿAdī b. Zayd's line uses *nakāl* for penalty; and the root carries the sense of binding back, since *al-nikl* and *al-ankāl* are fetters — named because by them a man is *yunkal*, held back; a heavy bridle is called *nakl* because it restrains the animal, and *nakala ʿan al-amr* is to refuse and refrain. *Al-tankīl* is to visit an enemy with a punishment that *deters* those behind them — that makes them shrink back [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī]. *Mawʿiẓah*, on the measure *mafʿilah*, is from *al-ittiʿāẓ*, taking heed and stopping short; al-Khalīl said *al-waʿẓ* is reminding of good in a way that softens the heart, and *al-ʿiẓah* is the noun — and **[Study Quran]** observes that *mawʿiẓah* can lean either way in the language, counsel and exhortation as in 2:275, 3:138 and 5:46, or reprimand and admonition. In the grammar, *nakalan* is the second object of "We made," and *mawʿiẓah* is joined to it [Qurṭubī].

**Context — what "it" refers to.** **[Ṭabarī]** lays out five positions. From Ibn ʿAbbās, two: that the pronoun stands for the *maskh*, the transformation itself — God made His punishment of them by metamorphosis a deterrent; or that it stands for *the fish*, though no fish had yet been mentioned, the context pointing to it. Others: the village whose people transgressed; the apes themselves; or the community that broke the Sabbath. And "what was before it and what was after it" divides similarly: Ibn ʿAbbās — those who came after, to be warned by God's punishment, and those who remained with them; al-Rabīʿ and Qatāda in one report — the sins that had gone before them and the fish they took; Ibn ʿAbbās in ʿIkrimah's report — the villages around; Mujāhid — their past sins and the sins that destroyed them; and in the fuller reading al-Zajjāj and Ibn ʿAṭiyyah preferred: the transformation was made a warning for what had gone before of their sins and for what would be done after it, so that people would fear the same fate. al-Naḥḥās chose Ibn ʿAbbās's "those present and those who come after" as closest to the meaning [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī] [Study Quran]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** takes the same sense: the village was made an example for the villages around it, and the story was preserved in revelation as a reminder to those who came later — as 46:27 says, "We destroyed the towns around you and turned the signs in various ways, that they might return."

**Belief & reflection.** **[Qurṭubī]** records al-Māwardī's answer to a natural question: the warning is for the world, so why were the reverent named? Because they alone take it, as against the obstinate disbeliever who passes by the sign and does not move; and al-Zajjāj read the clause as aimed at the community of Muḥammad ﷺ — that they should not tear down what God has made sacred, lest there fall on them what fell on the people of the Sabbath when they violated God's sanctity on their day. Ibn ʿAṭiyyah's point is worth carrying, that the wording includes every reverent person of every community [Qurṭubī]. The verse thus closes the story with a general law: God's punishments in history are signs for those who can read them, and what makes the difference is not intelligence but reverence. Two people pass the ruined town; one walks on, one stops and changes direction.


## 2:67

*"And when Mūsā said to his people, 'God commands you to slaughter a cow,' they said, 'Do you take us in mockery?' He said, 'I seek refuge in God from being among the ignorant.'"*

**Meaning.** The scene returns to the wilderness generation, this time for a command that tested far more than their appetites [Saʿdī] [Ṭabarī]. **[Saʿdī]** supplies the frame: a man had been killed among them, and they fell to disputing and accusing one another over his killer until the matter nearly produced a great evil; to expose the killer, Mūsā told them to slaughter a cow — and the duty at that moment was to obey at once without objection. They refused anything but objection: "Do you take us in mockery?" And the prophet answered that the ignorant man is the one who speaks words with no benefit in them and mocks people; the wise man counts mockery of another as one of the greatest defects, disgracing both religion and reason, and if one man has been favoured over another, the favour obliges gratitude to God and mercy to His servants.

**Context — the murder behind the command.** The reports vary in detail but agree on the shape. ʿUbaydah al-Salmānī: a man in Israel was childless, with substantial wealth and only a nephew to inherit him, so the nephew killed him and moved the body at night to another tribe's quarter; fighting nearly broke out, and their leaders said, "Will you kill one another while God's Messenger is among you?" They came to Mūsā, the command to slaughter a cow came down, and only after their repeated questions did they find the particular cow; the dead man was struck with a part of it and rose and named his killer — after which no killer was ever allowed to inherit [Ibn Kathīr] [Ṭabarī]. Abū al-ʿĀliyyah: a wealthy man with no son, killed by a relative who stood to inherit, then thrown at the crossroads; the murderer himself came to Mūsā asking him to ask God, and when the answer came they were astonished: "Do you take us in mockery?" [Ṭabarī]. **[Maʿārif]** records the version in *Mirqāt*: a man of Israel sought a girl in marriage, her father refused, and the suitor killed him and disappeared — and notes al-Kalbī's remark that no law on manslaughter had yet been revealed, which would place the incident before the Torah. **[Study Quran]** summarizes the same background from al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr, notes the resemblance to Numbers 19's red heifer — "without defect, in which there is no blemish and on which no yoke has been laid" — and observes that the Israelites' impertinent counter-questioning has no parallel in the Biblical account.

**Language.** *Huzuwan* is play and mockery; the rhyme "Umm Ṭaysalah has mocked me, saying she sees me penniless with nothing" — that is, she scoffed and made sport [Ṭabarī]. **[Saʿdī]** presses the word's weight: mockery is what the *jāhil* does. **[Study Quran]** explains the Qurʾānic sense of *jahl*: to be *jāhil* is not a mere lack of knowledge but a disposition against it — a pathological adherence to one's own way, the same vision that underlies the term *jāhiliyyah* for the age before Islam — and it cites 6:35, 11:46 and 12:33, where being "among the ignorant" is a state one fears and a warning one is given. **[Qurṭubī]** notes that "a cow" is the female noun, the male being *thawr*, like *nāqah* and *jamal* — though some hold *baqarah* to cover both sexes; and the root sense is splitting, since the cow splits the earth with ploughing; he also records Abū ʿAmr's reading *yaʾmurkum* with the *rāʾ*'s vowel lightened, and al-Mubarrad's rejection of a fully vowelless reading, since the *rāʾ* is a letter of inflection.

**Rulings.** **[Qurṭubī]** gathers the juristic matters the verse raises. The scholars agree that *dhabḥ* (slaughter at the throat) is preferable for sheep and goats, *naḥr* (the stab at the chest) for camels, and either is acceptable for cattle — though Mālik disapproved of the interchange without prohibiting it; Ibn al-Mundhir said he knew of no one who declared forbidden either manner of killing for the animal assigned to the other. And al-Māwardī gives the reason a cow was chosen rather than another animal: it was of the same kind as the calf they had worshipped, so that they would come to think little of what they had once magnified, and their obedience would expose what had truly been in their hearts. He adds a second wisdom: the living should be made to speak by the killing of a living creature, so that God's power would be shown more clearly in bringing things out from their opposites.

**Belief & reflection.** **[Ṭabarī]** draws the doctrine from the rebuke itself: mockery and play are not possible in a prophet when he conveys a command or a prohibition from God; their suspicion was itself a sin, since Mūsā was telling them that God had ordered it. So his answer is not a denial of the command but a disavowal of the character imputed to him — "I seek refuge with God from being among the ignorant," the fools who attribute to God what is false. And the verse marks the beginning of a pattern that will run through the cow's entire story: a people who could have obeyed with any cow, and who will instead question their way into a uniquely expensive animal — as the report puts it, had they taken the nearest cow it would have sufficed them. What looks like carefulness is, in the Qurʾān's reading, evasion in search of a way out.


## 2:68

*"They said, 'Call upon your Lord for us to make clear to us what she is.' He said, 'He says she is a cow neither old nor young, middling between that; so do what you are commanded.'"*

**Meaning.** The first of the three counter-questions, and the first narrowing of the command [Ṭabarī] [Ibn Kathīr] [Saʿdī]. **[Saʿdī]** reads the question as asking about her age and the answer as closing the door on further haggling: neither a grande dame nor a heifer, but between the two — *so do what you are commanded*; leave off severity and needless prying. **[Ibn Kathīr]** states the law of the passage: God had mentioned the stubbornness of the Children of Israel and their excessive questioning of their prophets, so when they made the matter hard, God made it harder for them; had they slaughtered any cow it would have sufficed, as Ibn ʿAbbās and ʿUbaydah said.

**Context — why this is rebuke, not diligence.** **[Ṭabarī]** explains that by now they knew and were settled in the fact that the command came from God, not from Mūsā's own initiative. God had commanded them to slaughter *a cow* — any cow they wished, without restricting them to a type or a class — and they asked for a description God had already spared them the burden of, out of roughness of character, coarseness of constitution and poor understanding, imposing on themselves what God had waived — posing riddles to the Prophet ﷺ. Ibn ʿAbbās put it in one word: they said it *testing him* (yataʿannatūnahu). And because they had also offended by suspecting Mūsā of mockery, God requited them by restricting what He had left open: "she is a cow neither old nor young." **[Qurṭubī]** agrees with the diagnosis and gives the criterion: this is *taʿnīt* — vexatious quibbling — and want of obedience; had they simply complied and slaughtered any cow, the purpose would have been achieved; they tightened the rope on themselves, so God tightened it on them, and al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī reported the like from the Prophet ﷺ.

**Language.** *Fāriḍ* is the aged female — *faraḍat tafriḍu furūḍan*, she grew old — the same root used of old rancour that never cools, as the verse "many a man of ancient grudge against me" [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī]. *Bakr*, with the *bāʾ* vowelled *kasrah*, is a female of the cattle or of human beings that has not been covered by a male; with a *fatḥah* it is a young camel [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī]. *ʿAwān* is the middle-aged one that has borne a belly or two, the strongest and finest of cattle — unlike horses, where middle age is past its best; the plural is *ʿūn* and *ʿuwun*, and the word is used of a second war after a first, "when a middling war conceives" [Qurṭubī]. In the grammar, *lā fāriḍun wa-lā bakrun* may be read as adjectives of *baqarah*, or as the predicate of an implied subject with the force of "she is not old," and the like pattern follows in *lā dhalūlun* and *lā tasqī al-ḥarth* [Qurṭubī].

**Rulings — the command and the law of abrogation.** **[Qurṭubī]** finds in the verse a juristic and a legal-theoretical point. As to the theory: the first command, "slaughter a cow," implied any cow at all; when the description was added, the first ruling was superseded before the act — a demonstration that abrogation may come before the time of performance, just as the alms due on thirty camels might first be a yearling she-camel and then be replaced by a two-year-old. As to the law of commands: *so do what you are commanded* is a renewal and reinforcement of the order, and a warning against the very thing they did not abandon; it shows that a command in itself carries obligation — the position of the jurists — and, on the majority view, that it requires immediate performance, since God later reproached them with "they nearly did not do it." Ibn Khuwayz Mindād argued the contrary, that obeying a command admits delay, since God did not rebuke them for the delay and the questioning. **[Ibn Kathīr]** collects the agreement on the description: every reported authority — Ibn ʿAbbās, Mujāhid, ʿIkrimah, Abū al-ʿĀliyyah, al-Suddī, al-Ḥasan, Qatāda, Wahb b. Munabbih and others — held her to be neither aged nor below the age of breeding; al-Ḍaḥḥāk from Ibn ʿAbbās said she was at the age when a cow is strongest and fittest.

**Reflection.** Every question they asked was plausible, and every answer cost them something. Had they gone out and slaughtered the first cow they met, the dead man would have spoken and the command would have been behind them; instead they asked what she was, and the answer took away the young and the old — and then the colour, and then the yoke. **[Study Quran]** records Ibn ʿAjībah's use of precisely this feature: the soul must be slaughtered so that the spiritual soul may live, and the best moment is when it is neither too old, its habits too deeply set for change, nor too young, feeling immortal and needing no change. The Qurʾān does not condemn asking questions as such; it condemns questions offered in place of obedience, the kind whose real content is "not yet."


## 2:69

*"They said, 'Call upon your Lord for us to make clear to us what her colour is.' He said, 'He says she is a yellow cow, bright is her colour, pleasing to the beholders.'"*

**Meaning.** The second question, and the second restriction. **[Ṭabarī]** is explicit that this too was *taʿannut* — a fresh round of quibbling after the first: God had narrowed them to a type but had not bound them to a colour, and they insisted on asking what they had no need to know, and were then bound to a colour as well. The answer names the animal exactly: a yellow cow, whose colour is *fāqiʿ*, delighting those who look at her.

**Language.** In *mā lawnuhā*, the *mā* is interrogative and the subject, which is why *lawn* is nominative; Ṭabarī explains at length that the verb "make clear to us" cannot govern the interrogative directly, any more than it can govern *ayy*, since *mā* and *ayy* gather up the scattered parts of a question — as when one says, "make clear to us whether this cow is black or yellow" [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī]. *Fāqiʿ* is intense and pure: the Arabs say *aṣfar fāqiʿ* for a concentrated yellow, as they say *aswad ḥālik*, *aḥmar qāniʾ*, *abyaḍ nāṣiʿ*, *akhḍar nāḍir*; al-Kisāʾī said *faqaʿa lawnuhā* is that its yellowness became unclouded. And the word wanders usefully: *al-ifqāʿ* is a bad state, *fawāqiʿ al-dahr* are its calamities, and *tafqīʿ* is cracking the fingers until the joints snap — which Ibn ʿAbbās forbade in prayer [Qurṭubī]. *Ṣafrāʾ* takes no *tanwīn* in either definite or indefinite use because of the inseparable *alif* of femininity, unlike forms ending in *hāʾ* [Qurṭubī].

**Reading — what yellow meant.** The majority of exegetes took it plainly: yellow in colour, and some added that even the horns and the hooves were yellow [Qurṭubī] [Ibn Kathīr]. al-Ḥasan and Ibn Jubayr limited it to the horns and hooves alone. And al-Ḥasan in one report said *ṣafrāʾ* here means *black* — supporting it with the poet's "those are my horses and those my riding camels; they are *ṣufr*, their young like raisins," and with 77:33, "as though they were yellow camels" — because the blackness of a black camel carries a yellow cast. **[Qurṭubī]** rejects it: the apparent sense is the soundest, and the fact that God reinforced the colour with *fāqiʿ* settles it, since *fāqiʿ* is a description specific to yellowness and the Arabs do not say *aswad fāqiʿ*. Ibn ʿAbbās, in al-ʿAwfī's report, described it as a deep yellowish white [Ibn Kathīr].

**"Pleasing to the beholders."** Wahb b. Munabbih said that if you looked at her skin you would think the sun's rays were coming out of it; Abū al-ʿĀliyyah, Qatāda and al-Rabīʿ said she pleased those who saw her — Abū al-ʿĀliyyah adding that it was in her bearing and her appearance, not her colour alone [Ibn Kathīr] [Qurṭubī]. **[Qurṭubī]** notes Ibn ʿAbbās's word that yellowness pleases the soul, and how far the early authorities carried the remark: he encouraged yellow sandals, and ʿAlī is reported to have said that one who wears yellow leather sandals has little worry, since God said *yellow, bright is her colour, pleasing to the beholders*; Ibn al-Zubrayr and Muḥammad b. Abī Kathīr disliked black sandals for the opposite reason, that they bring gloom. *(digression)* This is a mild example of a habit worth naming: a Qurʾānic description of a cow's colour is quoted as a sanction for footwear. The verse's subject is a specific animal in a specific command; the sandal reports show the tradition's ease with taking a word's pleasantness as a general sign, not a rule of dress. **[Ibn Kathīr]** closes with a comparison to the Torah's account of a red heifer in Numbers 19 and comments that the description there may be an error or that she was so deeply yellow as to appear dark or reddish — either way, God knows best.

**Reflection.** Two questions have now cost them a colour, and the third will cost them the yoke. The remarkable thing is that the answers are beautiful: whatever God chose was worth looking at, *pleasing to the beholders*, in a scene that ends with a corpse rising to name its killer. The command they were treating with such caution was the raw material of a resurrection. **[Ṭabarī]**'s diagnosis is the one to keep: the second question was also *taʿannut* — not a search for guidance, but the itch of a people who had heard the answer and wanted another one.


## 2:70

*"They said, 'Call upon your Lord for us to make clear to us what she is — for cows are much alike to us — and we shall, if God wills, be guided.'"*

**Meaning.** The third question, and the first softening. **[Jalālayn]** renders the question as asking whether she grazes free or is used for labour, and reads *cows are much alike to us* as their confession that the species is numerous and they had not found the one described. **[Saʿdī]** hears in the closing clause their admission that they had not reached what Mūsā intended, and their new vow: "and we shall, if God wills, be guided." **[Ṭabarī]** counts it a third instance of their ignorance, and gives the arithmetic of it: when they were first commanded to slaughter a cow, any cow falling under the name would have sufficed, for they had not been burdened with a description; then they asked, and were restricted by age; and having been given the age, had they slaughtered the cheapest cow of that age it would have sufficed, for they had not been restricted by colour; then they asked again, and the colour was fixed. The rope they were pulling was tied to their own necks.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** sets beside the story the Prophet's ﷺ warning to his own community: "Leave me alone as long as I leave you. Those before you were destroyed only by their many questions and their disagreement with their prophets. When I command you something, do it as much as you can; when I forbid you something, abstain from it." Ibn ʿAbbās: had they taken the nearest cow it would have sufficed them — but they made it hard, so God made it hard on them; and ʿUbaydah al-Salmānī: had they taken the cheapest cow it would have served. ʿIkrimah adds the other half: if the Israelites had taken any cow it would have sufficed them, and had it not been for their saying "if God wills," they would never have found her at all [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī]. **[Jalālayn]** reports the same as a hadith: had they not uttered the proviso, it would never have been made clear to them.

**Language & readings.** *Al-baqar* is a collective noun, which is why the verb is masculine, *tashābaha*; the plural forms are *bāqir*, *bāqūr*, *baqar* and *baqīr*, and the Arabs use the word as both masculine and feminine — which is what the variant readings reflect. al-Ḥasan and al-Aʿraj read *inna al-baqara tashshābaha* with the *tāʾ* assimilated into the *shīn* and the verb feminine; Mujāhid read it without the *alif* in the same form; Yaḥyā b. Yaʿmur read *inna al-bāqira yashshābahu*, masculine and assimilated; and the muṣḥaf of Ubayy has *tashābahat* with the *shīn* doubled, which Abū Ḥātim judged an error, since the *tāʾ* in this pattern is assimilated only in the imperfect [Qurṭubī]. As for the sense of the complaint, **Qurṭubī** gives a charming observation: the faces of cattle resemble one another — which is why the Prophet ﷺ described the trials of the end of time as coming "like the faces of cows," one looking like the next; and it was this very resemblance of the herd that the Israelites pleaded.

**Language — the exception.** *In shāʾa Allāh* is their exception, and **[Qurṭubī]** finds in it, coming at the last of their questions, a turning back and a measure of submission, and evidence of regret for not having simply complied. The ḥadīth runs: "Had they not made the exception, they would never have been guided to her." In the grammar, the sense is "and we are guided, if God wills" — the condition was brought forward out of concern for it, and the answer to *in* is the clause "we are guided" with its *inna*, as Sibawayh held, while al-Mubarrad took the answer to be an omitted clause [Qurṭubī].

**Reflection.** Something changes in this verse. Twice they had asked and received a narrower command in return; the third time they ask with a reason and end with a submission — "if God wills." It is not much, and **[Qurṭubī]** calls it no more than a turn of the head, but the Qurʾān records it, and the hadith says the whole finding of the cow hung on it. A story that began with suspicion of the prophet ends the round with a phrase of humility; and the fact that God's answer to their stubbornness was to specify her more rather than to abandon them is the mercy inside the tightening. They made the road narrow; He kept them on it.


## 2:71

*"He said, 'He says she is a cow not broken to the yoke, that does not till the earth nor water the tillage, sound, with no blemish on her.' They said, 'Now you have brought the truth.' So they slaughtered her, and they were near to not doing it."*

**Meaning.** The last specification, and the moment they finally obey [Jalālayn] [Ibn Kathīr] [Saʿdī]. **[Jalālayn]** gives the plain sense of the description — a cow not subdued for labour, neither plowing the earth nor carrying water for the crops, safe from faults and from the marks of toil, with no blemish of any colour but her own — and then the outcome: they searched and found her in the keeping of a boy most dutiful to his mother, bought her for her weight in gold, and slaughtered her, having very nearly not done so because of her cost. Ibn Kathīr's verdict on the delay is blunt: even after all the questions and answers, they were still reluctant, and the reason is that their aim all along was to be stubborn — which is what "they were near to not doing it" criticizes.

**Language.** *Lā dhalūl* is a cow not subdued by work: a ridden or laboured animal is *dābbah dhalūl*, and the noun is *al-dhill* with a *kasrah* — gentleness, the opposite of wildness; when it is said of a human being, *al-dhull* with a *ḍammah* is abasement [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī]. The majority read *lā dhalūl* in the nominative as a description of *baqarah*; Abū ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Sulamī read it in the accusative as a negation with an implied predicate — "she is not subdued" — and *lā hiya taṣqī al-ḥarth* is likewise possible [Qurṭubī]. *Tuthīru al-arḍ* is turning the soil for sowing, from *athāra*, to turn and search the ground — the same verb as 30:9, "and they turned the earth"; and the ḥadīth "stir up the Qurʾān, for in it is the knowledge of the former and the latter" carries the metaphor from soil to text [Qurṭubī]. Because she was wild and untouched, she could not have been described this way unless she had truly never been yoked — al-Ḥasan said plainly that she was *waḥshiyyah* [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī]. *Musallamah* is *mufʿalah* from *salāmah*, soundness. The exegetes split over what she was sound from: Mujāhid said sound from *shiyah*, that is, any marking of black or white — but **[Ṭabarī]** prefers Ibn ʿAbbās, Abū al-ʿĀliyyah and Qatāda, who said sound from defects and faults, arguing that if "sound" meant only freedom from contrary colours, then "with no blemish on her" would be redundant — whereas each phrase must carry its own weight: free of defects, and free of any colour-marking but her own.

**Rulings — description as specification.** **[Qurṭubī]** draws from this verse its most consequential legal principle: it is the strongest evidence that an animal may be defined by its attributes, and that when a thing is pinned down by description, advance purchase (*al-salam*) in it is valid. Mālik and his companions, al-Awzāʿī, al-Layth and al-Shāfiʿī held this, generalizing it to everything describable — for God described this cow in His Book with a description that stood in the place of pointing her out. He supports it with the ḥadīth in Muslim, "Let no woman describe another woman to her husband so that it is as though he were looking at her" — the Prophet ﷺ made description stand for sight — and with the fact that he made the blood-money of accidental killing a debt payable at a term, not immediately due, which refutes the Kufan position of Abū Ḥanīfah and his companions, al-Thawrī and al-Ḥasan b. Ṣāliḥ, that *salam* in livestock is invalid because an animal's walk, motion and condition cannot be captured. The rules of *salam* and its conditions, he says, belong to the verse of debt at the end of this Sūrah.

**Reflection.** "Now you have brought the truth," they say — as though truth had only just arrived. **[Saʿdī]** observes the irony: he had brought them the truth the first time, and any cow would have served; had they objected to nothing they would have performed the command with the first animal they met, and it was their tightening of the rope that produced the cow whose price was her weight in gold. And even then they almost did not. The verse leaves the reader with the whole anatomy of evasion: the questions, the narrowing, the costly animal, the near-failure — and the deed finally done. What was asked of them at the beginning was a cow; what they gave was almost everything but the obedience, and then the obedience too.


## 2:72

*"And when you slew a soul and cast the blame upon one another regarding it — and God is the One who brings out what you were concealing."*

**Meaning.** The verse returns to the beginning of the story to name the crime that produced the cow: a killing followed by a quarrel in which each party pushed the guilt onto another, and a concealment God then exposed [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī] [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** records the reported speech of the dispute — "you killed him" / "no, you killed him" — and al-Bukhārī's gloss that *iddāraʾtum* means "you disputed," as Mujāhid and ʿAṭāʾ al-Khurāsānī said.

**Language.** *Iddāraʾtum* is originally *tadāraʾtum*, on the pattern *tafāʿaltum*, from *al-darʾ*, which is crookedness and also repelling: the *tāʾ* was assimilated into the *dāl*, and since speech cannot begin with a quiescent assimilated letter, the connecting *alif* was added. The word thus means mutual pushing-back — each side thrusting the accusation away from itself and onto the other, which is why the exegetes gloss it simply: you differed, you quarrelled [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī]. **[Ṭabarī]**'s Arabic citations show the root's reach: a hard, crooked matter is *dhū al-darʾ*, and Ruʾbah's line has the poet pushing back every arrogant opponent. *Mukhrijun mā kuntum taktumūn*: "God is the One who brings out" — a nominative sentence, with the relative clause standing as the object, and the implied pronoun "you were concealing *it*" [Qurṭubī].

**Context — why the order is inverted.** **[Qurṭubī]** states the fact plainly: this passage is placed before the start of the story in recitation, though in meaning it comes first — as 18:1-2 can be read as "the Book, upright, and He made no crookedness in it," the phrases reordered. The two reports of the motive: a man had a beautiful daughter whom her paternal cousin wished to marry, the father refused, and the suitor killed him and carried him from one village to another, or laid him between two villages; or he killed him to inherit his wealth, being poor, and then laid the killing at the door of one of the tribes. ʿIkrimah's version gives the scene: the Israelites had a mosque with twelve gates, one for each tribe; a dead man was found in one of the quarters, and tribe accused tribe until they came to Mūsā disputing [Qurṭubī]. **[Maʿārif]** explains the narrative arrangement as deliberate: if the account had been given in chronological order, a reader might have thought that the murder was the sole point and the wrangling over the command no more than a way of filling out the story; by putting the objections first and the murder after, the Qurʾān gives both crimes equal emphasis — the killing and the concealment as one, and the needless questioning of a divine command as the other.

**Rulings — the killer and the inheritance.** **[Qurṭubī]** follows the story into the law it left behind. On the view that the motive was inheritance, ʿUbaydah al-Salmānī says that from that time an intentional killer did not inherit; Ibn ʿAbbās said this man killed his uncle to inherit him; and Ibn ʿAṭiyyah notes that our law came with the same rule. Mālik in the *Muwaṭṭaʾ* reports that the case of Uḥayḥah b. al-Julāḥ and his uncle was the reason a killer does not inherit, and that Islam confirmed it, as it confirmed many pre-Islamic practices. The scholars agree that the intentional killer inherits neither the blood-money nor the estate — only a deviant band of innovators held otherwise. As for the accidental killer, Mālik, al-Awzāʿī, Abū Thawr and al-Shāfiʿī hold that he inherits from the estate but not from the blood-money, since he did not intend the killing in order to take the wealth; Abū Ḥanīfah, al-Thawrī and al-Shāfiʿī in another report hold that neither intentional nor accidental killers inherit anything of estate or blood-money, a position reported from ʿUmar, ʿAlī, Zayd, Shurayḥ, Ṭāwūs, al-Shaʿbī and al-Nakhaʿī. **[Qurṭubī]**'s own preference is Mālik's.

**Reflection.** The arrangement of these verses is itself a lesson: the Sūrah shows the murder last, as a hinge rather than a preface. Men concealed a body between villages and thought the matter settled; and the wheels that God set in motion to expose them were a command to slaughter a cow, three rounds of impertinent questions, and a price in gold. The verse's last clause is a warning fit for every case of concealment — "God is the One who brings out what you were concealing" — not as an abstract attribute, but as the specific fact of this story. He brought it out through the evasions themselves.


## 2:73

*"So We said, 'Strike him with part of it.' Thus does God give life to the dead and show you His signs, that you may understand."*

**Meaning.** The command that closed the story, and the argument drawn from it: a dead man revived by contact with a slaughtered cow, and a general law announced under a particular event — *thus* does God give life to the dead [Jalālayn] [Ibn Kathīr] [Maʿārif]. **[Jalālayn]** records the tradition that he was struck with her tongue or her tail, came back to life and named his killer — two of his cousins — then died again; the killers were disinherited and later put to death. And the purpose clause is drawn out: God shows His proofs so that they may understand, reflecting that the One able to revive a single soul is able to revive the multitude — and then believe.

**Context — which part of the cow?** **[Ṭabarī]** gathers the reports: Mujāhid, ʿIkrimah, ʿUbaydah and Qatāda said the thigh; al-Suddī the piece of flesh between the shoulders; Abū al-ʿĀliyyah a bone; Ibn Zayd one of her limbs. Then he gives his own verdict, which is a model of restraint: the sound position is that God commanded them to strike the slain man with some part of the cow so that he would live; neither the verse nor any report that constitutes proof indicates which part, and it is possible it was the thigh, the tail, the shoulder-joint or any other limb; ignorance of the specific part neither harms nor does knowledge of it benefit, so long as one affirms that they struck him with part of the cow after slaughtering her and God revived him. Nor is the purpose left obscure: the command was given so that he would live and inform Mūsā and the disputants of his killer — the Qurʾān omits the explicit statement, leaving it to be inferred from the context [Ṭabarī]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** draws the same line: any part of the cow would have produced the miracle, and God left the detail vague for our benefit, so we leave it vague.

**Belief — the argument from the event.** **[Maʿārif]** raises the two questions a reader naturally asks — why the resurrection should depend on contact with a piece of flesh when God could revive him directly, and why the killer could not simply be revealed — and answers: God is omnipotent and acts by no compulsion, but all His acts proceed from wisdom; He alone knows the reason of what He does, and the law does not oblige us to find a rationale for every divine act. **[Ibn Kathīr]** uses the verse to list the Sūrah's resurrections: the raising after death in 2:56, this cow, the thousands who fled death (2:243), the man who passed a ruined village (2:259), Abraham and the four birds (2:260) — and the reviving of the dead earth elsewhere — all of them alerts that bodies will again be made whole after they have rotted. **[Study Quran]** places this among the Qurʾānic reanimations: Abraham and the birds (2:260), Jesus and the clay bird (3:49), the man revived after a hundred years (2:259), and the dust of the messenger that animated the golden calf (20:96); it records the ḥadīth in which the Prophet ﷺ answered the question of how God revives the dead — "Have you ever passed by a barren valley and then passed it again when it was verdant?" "Yes." "Even so is the Resurrection" — and notes the sufi reading of al-Qushayrī: "Whoever desires that his heart be given life must sacrifice his soul; whoever sacrifices his soul with effort gives life to his heart and the lights of vision." It also notes that Deuteronomy 21:1-9 has an ordinance of a cow and an unavenged killing, but that the parallels differ: there a general law is prescribed, here an event narrated.

**Rulings — testimony from the dead.** **[Maʿārif]** adds a juristic caution: in this incident the murdered man's statement was accepted as evidence for condemning the killer, because God had informed Mūsā by revelation that he would speak the truth; otherwise no one may be declared guilty of murder without the evidence the Sharīʿah has laid down. The miracle did not abrogate the law of proof; it stood within a revelation that guaranteed the fact.

**Reflection.** "That you may understand" — the verse assigns the miracle a purpose beyond the verdict on one murderer. The whole story has been a study in a people who would not take a word as a word; and God's answer to that temper was to make them watch. They disputed over a corpse and were told to slaughter a cow; they quarrelled over a command and were given the animal's description in three stages; and then they saw the dead stand up and name his cousin. Every evasion they manufactured turned out to be a step in the demonstration. What they had wanted was to bury the case; what they got was a proof of the Resurrection, and the cow was only ever the means.


## 2:74

*"Then your hearts hardened after that, so that they are like stones, or harder still. For among stones are those from which rivers gush forth, and among them are those that split and water comes out of them, and among them are those that fall down out of fear of God. And God is not heedless of what you do."*

**Meaning.** The verdict on the whole sequence: after the signs — the sea, the water from the rock, the man raised from the dead — their hearts did not soften; they hardened [Ibn Kathīr] [Saʿdī] [Jalālayn]. **[Saʿdī]** describes the hardening as thickening and stiffness such that admonition no longer reaches: *after that*, meaning after God had shown them favours and signs which by rights should have produced tenderness and compliance. **[Ibn Kathīr]** ties it to 57:16, where the believers are warned not to become like those who received the Scripture before them, whose hearts hardened when the term was prolonged for them, and notes that the hardness is described as beyond that of stone, since stone can still be split by water, while these hearts could no longer be softened at all.

**Language.** *Al-qaswah* is hardness, rigidity, dryness — an expression for the heart's emptiness of turning back and of submission to God's signs [Qurṭubī] [Ṭabarī]. *Aw* in "like stones or harder": the grammarians gave several accounts [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī] — that it carries the sense of *wa* ("and harder"), as in "do not obey among them a sinner or a disbeliever" (76:24) and 37:147, "we sent him to a hundred thousand or more"; that it means *bal* ("rather"), as in "or you are more beautiful in the eye" — meaning "rather, you are"; that it is *ibhām*, deliberate vagueness addressed to the hearer, as Abū al-Aswad al-Duʾalī used it, and as 34:24, "we or you are upon guidance or in clear error," a verse the poet was asked about: "Did you doubt?" and he cited the Qurʾān back; and that it presents two alternatives of which both are true — some of them had hearts like stone and some harder. **[Ṭabarī]** settles it: there is no doubt in God's report; the doubt is theirs and others' who know their case — their hearts are, *in the eyes of those who deal with them*, like stone or harder. **[Saʿdī]** notes sharply that the *aw* here is not *bal*, and that Saʿdī reads it as marking that their hearts do not fall short of the hardness of stone — while stone, unlike iron and lead, does not melt in fire. *Yaẓẓaqqaq* is originally *yatashaqqaq*, the *tāʾ* assimilated into the *shīn* [Jalālayn] [Qurṭubī].

**Context & āthār.** Ibn ʿAbbās, in al-ʿAwfī's report: when the murdered man stood up and named his nephews, and then died again, the heirs said, "By God, we did not kill him," denying the truth while knowing it — and it was then that their hearts became like stones [Qurṭubī] [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Qurṭubī]** records the ḥadīths the verse has been read with: from Ibn ʿUmar, in al-Tirmidhī, "Do not speak much without the remembrance of God, for much speech without the remembrance of God hardens the heart, and the people farthest from God are those with hard hearts"; and from Anas in al-Bazzār, "Four things are of wretchedness: dryness of the eye, hardness of the heart, length of hope, and greed for the world."

**Belief — do stones fear?** **[Ibn Kathīr]** records that some took the falling of stones out of fear of God as metaphor, while al-Rāzī, al-Qurṭubī and other leading scholars held that there is no need for that, since God creates this humility in stones just as He informed us that the heavens, the earth and the mountains declined the Trust and feared it (33:72), that the stars and the trees prostrate (55:6), that the mountains answered with Dāwūd (34:10), and that a mountain would have been humbled and split by the Qurʾān (59:21). The Prophet ﷺ said of Uḥud, "a mountain that loves us and we love it," and in Muslim, "I know a stone in Makkah that used to greet me before I was sent, and I know it still"; and the palm trunk wept for the remembrance it used to hear. **[Study Quran]** notes the connection the commentators draw with God's disclosure to the mountain at Mūsā's request (7:143), the three kinds of stone read as three human types — those who weep much, those who weep little, and those who weep inwardly [Ibn Kathīr], and al-Qushayrī's reading of the verse as a rebuke to people whose inward state has hardened after they had once known spiritual openings. **[Maʿārif]** frames it as a hierarchy: stones that pour out rivers benefit creation greatly, stones that split and give some water benefit less, stones that give nothing at least respond to God with fear — and the hearts addressed come out below all three, since they neither benefit others nor tremble.

**Method — on Israelite material.** **[Saʿdī]** closes the passage with a note that belongs to the discipline of tafsīr itself: many commentators filled their books with Israelite stories, applied Qurʾānic verses to them, and made them the explanation of God's Book, citing the ḥadīth "narrate from the Children of Israel — there is no blame." His position: such reports may be transmitted *as reports*, kept separate and not hung upon the verses, but it is not permissible to make them the tafsīr of God's Book when they are not authentically established from the Messenger ﷺ — for their status is that of the instruction, "Neither believe the People of the Book nor disbelieve them," that is, they are doubtful, whereas the Qurʾān must be believed in with certainty as to both its words and meanings; a doubtful story cannot be made the certain sense of a divine verse. *(digression)* The point is worth carrying in this Sūrah precisely because the Sūrah's Israelite narratives generated so much of that material; the exegetes' stories are recorded here with the label where they are reported and not relied upon.

**Reflection.** The comparison is exact and unflattering. Stone is the proverbially dead thing; and yet four kinds of stone in the verse do what the human heart would not: they carry water, they split open, they fall in awe. The heart's privilege is that it can be moved by mercy and command; the heart's curse is that it can refuse so long, and so completely, as to come out below the mountain. And the verse ends not with a rhetorical flourish but with a fact held over the reader's head — *God is not heedless of what you do* — which is a comfort to the oppressed and a sentence on the comfortable.


## 2:75

*"Do you then hope that they will believe you, when a party of them used to hear the Word of God and then distort it, after they had understood it, knowingly?"*

**Meaning.** The verse turns from the history to its consequence for the Muslims' expectations: after a record like this, eagerness for their faith is misplaced [Ṭabarī] [Saʿdī] [Jalālayn]. **[Saʿdī]** reads it as a deliberate cutting-off of hope: do not set your hearts on their belief, for their character does not justify it — people who distort the speech of God after understanding it, assigning it meanings God never intended and passing the result off as from God, are people who block others from His way with the very Book they claim as their honour; how then should faith in you be hoped for from them? **[Jalālayn]** notes that the interrogative *a-fa-taṭmaʿūn* is one of rejection — "do not be eager, for they disbelieved before."

**Context — who heard the Word.** **[Qurṭubī]** and **[Study Quran]** record the two main readings. One holds the address to the Prophet's community, and specifically to the Anṣār, who were keen for the Jews to accept Islam because of the pacts and neighbourly ties between them; Ibn ʿAbbās held it addressed to the Prophet ﷺ alone, with the sense: do not grieve over their rejection of you, for they are of the same bad stock as those who came before. *Word of God* is taken as the Torah, which they heard from the prophets and then distorted — from al-Suddī and Ibn Zayd, who describes the trade: they made the lawful unlawful and the unlawful lawful, the true false and the false true; if a man with a grievance came with a bribe they brought out the Book of God for him, and if a wrongdoer came with a bribe they brought out the other book that made him right — which is why the Qurʾān elsewhere says to them, "Do you enjoin righteousness on people and forget yourselves while you recite the Scripture?" (2:44) [Ṭabarī] [Ibn Kathīr]. Others hold that the party are the seventy men Mūsā chose, who were said to have gone up the Mount and heard God's word directly, and who on returning reported it to the people altered what they had heard [Qurṭubī] [Ibn Kathīr] [Maʿārif] [Study Quran]. **[Qurṭubī]** finds weakness in that report: whoever says the seventy heard what Mūsā heard has erred and has erased Mūsā's distinction of being spoken to directly; al-Suddī's version instead has them unable to bear the hearing, their minds confused, asking that Mūsā hear and repeat it to them, and after they left, a group of them substituted what they had received through their prophet. **[Maʿārif]** holds both possibilities open: the distortion may have been in the words themselves, in the sense, or in both.

**Language & the meaning of taḥrīf.** *Ṭamaʿ* is hope and desire — *ṭamiʿa fīhi ṭamaʿan wa-ṭamāʿiyatan* — and the root reaches into strange corners: *al-ṭamaʿ* is also a soldier's stipend, the commander "orders their *aṭmāʿ*," their rations; and a woman *miṭmāʿ* is one who raises desire and grants nothing [Qurṭubī]. *Farīq* is a collective noun like *ṭāʾifah*, with no singular from its own form, pluralized *afriqah* and *afriqāʾ* [Qurṭubī] [Ṭabarī]. *Taḥrīf* is from *ḥarf*, a side or edge: to *ḥarrafa* a word is to turn it to a side, to slant it — the same idea as *inḥirāf*, deviation [Study Quran]. **[Ṭabarī]** notes what hangs on the phrase "after they had understood it": they were not misled or forgetful; the distortion was of the meaning, done with comprehension. **[Study Quran]** lays out the long debate honestly: whether the People of the Book altered the transmitted text of Scripture or slanted its interpretation while keeping the text intact. The earlier commentators preferred the second; al-Rāzī, commenting on 3:78, doubted that prophecies concerning the Prophet ﷺ had been physically removed, since that would require collusion on a grand scale with a well-known text, though on 2:75 he allowed that with a less widely circulated text it would not be impossible; Ibn Ḥazm later epitomized the view that the text itself was changed. **[Maʿārif]** adds the wider principle at work in the address: the Jews of the Prophet's own time may not have committed these particular acts, but since they did not abhor the deeds of their forebears, they are counted as their counterparts.

**Reflection.** The verse is a piece of pastoral realism. The Companions were burning to see their Jewish neighbours accept the truth — a good desire, and God does not rebuke the desire, only the expectation built on it. Faith is not something a community grants to another out of goodwill; and men who have learned to bend the meaning of revelation for a bribe are not bent straight by the enthusiasm of the ones they are bending it against. **[Study Quran]** notes the parallel admonitions to the Prophet ﷺ not to consume himself with grief over those who will not believe (18:6, 35:8), and al-Qushayrī's application of the verse to those who, having once known a spiritual opening, relapse into formality and lore — a warning that the *taḥrīf* condemned here can recur in a heart long after the Book on its shelf has stopped being touched.


## 2:76

*"And when they meet those who believe they say, 'We believe'; and when they are alone with one another they say, 'Do you tell them of what God has disclosed to you, that they may dispute with you about it before your Lord? Do you not understand?'"*

**Meaning.** The verse exposes the two faces of the Jewish hypocrites: a confession in public and a rebuke in private [Ṭabarī] [Saʿdī] [Jalālayn]. **[Saʿdī]** follows the movement: having established that no hope should be pinned on these people, God now describes the party among them who were openly hypocritical — they displayed belief with their tongues while their hearts held nothing of it; and when they were alone with their own, they told each other to stop disclosing what God had opened to them, because the admission would become a proof in the Muslims' hands, to be used against them before their Lord. "Do you not understand?" — that is, do you have no reason that would make you abandon a course that supplies your opponents with evidence against you?

**Context & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** records the reports. A group of the Jews would say to the Prophet ﷺ when they met him, "We believe," and say among themselves, "Do you tell them what God has commanded you?" — and the others would answer, "We are only mocking them and laughing." Abū al-ʿĀliyyah: they were told-off for disclosing what God had sent down in their Book concerning the description of Muḥammad ﷺ. Ibn Isḥāq's report from Ibn ʿAbbās gives the politics of it: they would say, "We believe in your companion — but he was sent to you specifically"; and in private they warned one another, "Do not tell the Arabs about this, for you used to seek victory through him over them, and he has come from among them"; and God exposed them: do you acknowledge that he is a prophet, knowing the covenant taken from you to follow him, while he tells you that this is the Prophet we were waiting for and finding in our Book? Deny him and acknowledge nothing to them. al-Suddī: these were people of the Jews who believed and then turned hypocrites. **[Qurṭubī]** gives a different specification of the disclosure: some of them had accepted Islam and then played the hypocrite, and they would tell the Muslim Arabs what their fathers had been punished with — so the Jews said, "Do you tell them what God has judged against you, so that they can say, 'We are nobler before God than you'?" Ibn ʿAbbās and al-Suddī said this. And **[Qurṭubī]** records from Mujāhid's material the occasion at Khaybar: ʿAlī heard them reviling the Prophet ﷺ and turned back; the Prophet ﷺ said, "I think you heard them revile me — had they seen me they would stop"; then he went out to them and, when they saw him, they fell silent, and he said: "Have you broken the treaty, brothers of apes and swine? God has disgraced you and brought down His vengeance on you!" They answered, "You were not ignorant, Muḥammad; do not act ignorantly toward us. Who told you this? This report can only have come from among us." **[Maʿārif]** gives the plain historical setting: some Jews, seeing the growing power of the Muslims in Madīnah, pretended to accept Islam to win their favour and would now and then reveal that the Torah had given glad tidings of the Prophet ﷺ and mentioned the Qurʾān; when they met the Jews who openly held to Judaism, they admitted they were only deceiving the Muslims, and the others reprimanded them for giving away what they themselves were at pains to conceal, since those verses could be used to defeat them in argument.

**Language.** *Laqū* is originally *laqiyū*; *khalā* is from *khalaw*, the *wāw* turned to *alif* because of its own vowel and the *fatḥah* before it [Qurṭubī]. *Fataḥa* here means "judged" and "decreed": *al-fatḥ* with the Arabs is judgement and ruling — "Our Lord, decide (*iftaḥ*) between us and our people with truth, for You are the best of deciders" (7:89); and *al-fattāḥ*, the judge, is a Yemeni usage, because a judge vindicates the wronged against the wrongdoer; *al-fatḥ* is also victory, hence "they used to seek victory (*yastaftiḥūn*) over those who disbelieved" (2:89) [Qurṭubī]. *Li-yuḥājjūkum* is governed by the *lām* of purpose, the *nūn* dropped in the accusative; some Arab tribes open the *lām* of purpose, and al-Akhfash said the *fatḥ* is the original [Qurṭubī]. *Al-ḥujjah* is speech that stands upright — the same root gives *maḥajjat al-ṭarīq*, the middle of the road; "I argued with a man and overcame him" is *ḥajajtuhu*, and in the ḥadīth, "so Adam got the better of Mūsā in argument." *ʿInda rabbikum* means in the Hereafter, as in "then on the Day of Resurrection before your Lord you will dispute with one another" (39:31), or it may mean "concerning your Lord" [Qurṭubī]. On *a-fa-lā taʿqilūn*: it may be spoken by the rabbis to their followers, or it may be God addressing the believers — do you not understand that the Children of Israel will not believe while in these conditions? — and 2:77 follows as a further rebuke with the same interrogative force [Qurṭubī].

**Reflection.** The verse is a study in a particularly self-defeating kind of cleverness. They could see the case against them being assembled from their own scriptures, and their strategy was to keep the documents away from the prosecution — while still greeting the Prophet ﷺ with "we believe" in the street. The Qurʾān's question is not moral but practical: *do you not understand?* A man who admits the truth in daylight and forbids the admission at night is not managing an argument; he is losing it twice, and adding to the loss the reputation of one who cannot be trusted in either room. **[Study Quran]** notes the parallel in 2:14, where the same double behaviour is exposed among the Prophet's antagonists, and 3:119; the Sūrah keeps returning to the divide between the tongue and the heart, and keeps saying that God's knowledge closes it.


## 2:77

*"Do they not know that God knows what they hide and what they disclose?"*

**Meaning.** One sentence, and the whole stratagem of the previous verse collapses [Ṭabarī] [Jalālayn] [Saʿdī]. **[Jalālayn]** notes that the interrogative is affirmative in force and that the inserted *wāw* marks the connection: God knows what they conceal and what they proclaim in this matter and in every matter, and the question is meant to bring them to desist. **[Saʿdī]** puts it as a rebuke of their reasoning: they imagined that concealing their belief among themselves would keep the proof from reaching the believers — and that was an error and a great ignorance, since God knows their secret and their open conduct and will expose to His servants what they are.

**Context.** **[Ṭabarī]** identifies who is being addressed: the Jews who reproached their own brothers for saying "we believe" to the believers and for telling them what their books contained about the Prophet ﷺ and his mission. God's question is aimed at those very reproachers — do they not know that He knows what they hide when they are alone, their disbelief and their mutual blame, and what they announce to Muḥammad ﷺ and his companions when they meet them, their *āmamnā* spoken as hypocrisy and deception? And he cites Qatāda and Abū al-ʿĀliyyah to the same effect: the hidden thing was their rejection of Muḥammad ﷺ while they found him written in their own Scripture, and the open thing was their saying to the believers, "We believe," to please them. **[Ibn Kathīr]** adds al-Ḥasan's account, which specifies the secret as the private council in which they forbade each other to convey to the companions of Muḥammad ﷺ what God had revealed in their Book — fearing it would be used against them before their Lord. **[Maʿārif]** draws the consequence: since God is omniscient, it makes no difference at all whether the hypocrites conceal their disbelief from the Muslims or whether the other group suppresses the Torah's testimony about the Prophet ﷺ — God Himself has informed the Muslims of both, in the Qurʾān. **[Study Quran]** notes only that the theme of God's knowledge of the hidden and the open is ubiquitous in the Qurʾān — 21:110, 24:29, 27:74, 28:69, 33:54, 36:76 — and that it connects directly to the conduct exposed in the preceding verse.

**Language.** The majority read *yaʿlamūn* with the *yāʾ* — "do they not know" — while Ibn Muḥayṣin read it with the *tāʾ*, addressing the believers: "do you not know"; and the *wāw* of *a-wa-lā* is the *wāw* of connection with what precedes, the *hamzah* of interrogation standing before it [Qurṭubī] [Jalālayn].

**Reflection.** The verse is the Sūrah's recurring answer to the religious double life: there is no private room. Men can manage their reputation with the community, and can even manage what their own colleagues know, but the disclosure has already been published in the one register that matters, and revelation keeps opening it in front of them. What the hypocrites feared in this scene — that the Muslims would have a proof before their Lord — was true, and the proof was not the private council; it was the One who heard it.


## 2:78

*"And among them are the unlettered who know nothing of the Book but fancies, and they only conjecture."*

**Meaning.** Having described the learned who distort and the hypocrites who conceal, the verse turns to the third class: the ordinary people who do not know the Book at all [Saʿdī] [Jalālayn] [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Saʿdī]** draws the picture of the whole community thus: some of them are scholars who distort; some are hypocrites; and among them are the unlettered masses who have no share of God's Book beyond recitation — no knowledge of what the earlier generations truly held, nothing but conjectures and inherited loyalties to their scholars — so that the scholars stick to their misguidance and the masses merely follow them without insight, and no hope should be placed in either group. **[Jalālayn]** renders *amānī* as the lies handed down to them by their leaders, which they relied on, and notes that in rejecting the Prophet's mission and in their other fabrications they have only conjecture, no firm knowledge.

**Language.** *Ummī* is one who neither writes nor reads — related to the *ummah* in its sense of a people left in its natural state, not having learned writing and reading from the mother — and the Prophet's ﷺ word belongs here: "We are an unlettered *ummah*; we do not write and we do not calculate," continuing with the month being such-and-such [Qurṭubī] [Ṭabarī] [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Ibn Kathīr]** notes that *ummī* was among the Prophet's own descriptions, and cites 29:48: "You did not read any book before it, nor write it with your right hand," and 62:2, "He it is who sent among the unlettered ones a messenger from among themselves." **[Qurṭubī]** lists the other views: Ibn ʿAbbās, in one report, said they were a people who accepted no prophet and no revealed book and wrote books themselves claiming they were from God; ʿIkrimah and al-Ḍaḥḥāk said they were the Christians of the Arabs; ʿAlī said they were the Magians; and Abū ʿUbaydah said they were called *ummiyyūn* because the Book was sent down upon them, as if related to the "mother of the Book" — making the phrase mean "among them are the People of the Book who do not know the Book." Qurṭubī's own preference is the plain view: those of the Jews who could not write or read. As for *amānī*, the word carries three senses in Arabic [Qurṭubī]: mere wish and hope; recitation — for *tamannā* can mean "to recite," as in 22:52, "when he recited, Satan cast into his recitation," and Kaʿb b. Mālik's line, "he recited the Book of God in the first of the night"; and falsehood, as when ʿUthmān said, "I have not *tamannaytu* since I accepted Islam," meaning, I have not lied. *Illā* here has the sense of *lākin*, "but" — an exception severed from the preceding clause, as in 4:157, "they have no knowledge of it except following conjecture" [Qurṭubī]. And *wa-in hum illā yaẓunnūn*: Mujāhid said the mufassirīn differ — some said it means "they lie," others that they hold evil and false ideas about God [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Study Quran]** adds that *amānī* holds both the sense of hearsay, what their scholars told them, and the sense of invented notions such as "the Fire shall not touch us except for a number of days" (2:80) or "none will enter Paradise unless he be a Jew" (2:111).

**Belief — the three kinds of ẓann.** **[Maʿārif]** pauses over the closing word, because a doctrine has been built on misreading it. The Qurʾān uses *ẓann* in three ways: for perfect certainty — "those who are certain that they will meet their Lord" (2:46); for the greatest likelihood — "he said to the one he thought would be released" (12:42); and for a fanciful opinion unsupported by argument, or contrary to a valid one — as in this verse. It is *only the third* that the Qurʾān condemns; the first two must be followed, and the decisions of the Sharīʿah that are described as resting on *ẓann* rest on the second sense. Those who take every Qurʾānic condemnation of *ẓann* and use it to reject all juristic conclusions built on probability have misunderstood the Book they are quoting.

**Reflection.** The verse describes a structure that outlives the community it names: specialists who manipulate, hired interpreters who sell, and a majority whose religion is a set of pleasant assurances they have never checked. Their distinguishing mark is not that they disbelieve — they hold the Book in their hands — but that what fills the place of knowledge in them is *amānī*: wishes, hearsay, stories. The Qurʾān does not call them wicked, only *yaẓunnūn*: guessing. And the guess, in the previous verses, has already been shown to be enough to harden a heart.


## 2:79

*"So woe to those who write the Book with their own hands and then say, 'This is from God,' to sell it for a paltry price. So woe to them for what their hands have written, and woe to them for what they earn."*

**Meaning.** The verse names the crime of the scholarly class directly: fabrication of scripture for profit, with the woe doubled — once for the writing, once for the gain [Jalālayn] [Saʿdī]. **[Saʿdī]** spells out what the double woe covers: they combined the making-public of falsehood with the concealing of the truth, and they did it knowingly; and their motive was to buy with it a paltry price. All of this world from its first day to its last is a paltry price — and they had made their falsehood a snare with which to hunt what was in people's hands. Then he counts the two wrongs: they confused people in their religion, and they took their wealth unjustly, indeed by the most futile of falsehood, which is worse than seizing it by force or theft.

**Language.** *Wayl* is the word the verse hangs over them, and the exegetes report much about it. From ʿUthmān b. ʿAffān, a report that it is a mountain of fire; from Abū Saʿīd, from the Prophet ﷺ, that it is a valley in Hell into which the disbeliever falls for forty autumns before reaching its bottom; from Abū ʿIyāḍ and Sufyān, that it is what flows from the pus of the people of the Fire at the bottom of Hell, or a cistern there; from others a gate of Hell; and from Ibn ʿAbbās, plainly, the hardship of torment [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī]. **[Ṭabarī]**'s own presentation leaves the lexical sense in the foreground, and **[Study Quran]** records that Ibn Kathīr disputed the "valley in Hell" report, while noting the other views: that it is a mountain of blood and pus, that it is the word the punished will cry out, or that it is the cry of the disbelievers in the Fire. In the language: al-Khalīl said *wayl* is the severity of evil; al-Aṣmaʿī said *wayl* is grief and *wayḥ* is mercy; Sibawayh said *wayl* is for one who has fallen into destruction while *wayḥ* is a rebuke to one about to fall into it; Ibn ʿArafah said *wayl* is sorrow, and the man who cries *wayl* is *mutawayyil*; al-Farrāʾ took the original to be *way* — "sorrow" — to which the Arabs attached the *lām* and then inflected it as a noun [Qurṭubī]. In the grammar, *fa-waylun* is nominative as a subject, and an indefinite noun may begin a sentence here because it carries the sense of an invocation — "may woe be theirs"; al-Akhfash allowed the accusative by an implied verb, "God has imposed woe upon them" [Qurṭubī].

**Context — what was written.** **[Jalālayn]** identifies the culprits as those who altered the description of the Prophet ﷺ in the Torah, and the verse of stoning, and other matters, rewriting them differently from the way they were revealed; the price they took was the bribe — the *rishwah*, plural *rishan*. **[Study Quran]** reports the exegetes' accounts: some Jews wrote something down and pawned it off on the ignorant Arabs for a small profit; and another account links the verse with the alteration of the prophecies foretelling the coming of Muḥammad ﷺ, so that the descriptions in their book were made to point elsewhere.

**Belief — the reach of the verse.** **[Saʿdī]** carries a passage from Ibn Taymiyyah applying these verses to the community of the Qurʾān itself, and it deserves to be quoted in substance: God condemned those who distort words from their places — and that includes anyone who loads the Book and the Sunnah onto the innovations he has founded; He condemned those who know nothing of the Book but fancies — and that includes anyone who abandons pondering the Qurʾān and knows only the recitation of its letters; and it includes one who writes a book with his own hand contrary to God's Book, aiming at some worldly gain, and says, "It is from God" — saying of his invention, "this is the Sharīʿah and the religion," "this is the meaning of the Book and the Sunnah," "this is the rationale of the Salaf and the imams," "these are the fundamentals of religion which must be believed"; and it includes one who conceals what he has of the Book and the Sunnah lest his opponent use it against him in a matter of truth. These things, he says, are very many among the people of caprice, both in general and in detail.

**Reflection.** Three categories have now passed in review: the scholars who distort knowingly, the masses who guess, and the ones who manufacture pages and sell them. The verse's economics are its sharpest point: they wrote the book themselves — the ink and the hand were theirs — and then charged people the price of revelation for it. And what did they get? A *paltry* price, the Qurʾān says, deliberately belittling the whole treasury of this world; they sold the Hereafter of a nation at the rate of a bribe. **[Saʿdī]**'s use of Ibn Taymiyyah removes the story from its ancient setting without softening it: the same three dangers — distorting, guessing, and writing for gain — reappear in every community that inherits a book, and the last of them, the manufacture of religious texts and rationales for a price, is the one that has always done the most damage with the most respectable appearance.


## 2:80

*"And they said, 'The Fire will not touch us except for days numbered.' Say, 'Have you taken a covenant with God, so that God will not break His covenant? Or do you speak of God what you do not know?'"*
