**Meaning.** The verse sets their claim against the only two things that could make it true [Saʿdī] [Ibn Kathīr] [Jalālayn]. **[Saʿdī]** puts the charge first: having listed their ugly deeds, the Qurʾān now shows them clearing themselves of guilt — claiming safety from God's punishment and success in His reward, with the Fire touching them only for a few days counted on the fingers. They combined wrongdoing with security. And their claim can stand on one of two possibilities and no third: either they took a covenant with God — a promise of salvation through faith in Him, His messengers and obedience to Him, which is never altered or exchanged — or they are inventing against God, in which case their exposure and punishment are worse. Their history settles which: they disbelieved many prophets, killed some of them, held back from obedience and broke their covenants; so they are of the second class, and speaking about God without knowledge is among the gravest of forbidden things and the most hideous of uglinesses.

**Context — the number they claimed.** **[Ṭabarī]** gathers the reports. Ibn ʿAbbās, Abū al-ʿĀliyyah and Qatāda: forty days or nights, corresponding to the time in which they worshipped the calf — "God swore to punish us for forty nights and then bring us out," Abū al-ʿĀliyyah said, and God gave them the lie. al-Suddī adds the belief built on it: after forty nights, when the Fire had eaten their sins and cleansed them, a caller would cry, "Bring out every circumcised one of the sons of Israel" — and that, he says, is why they circumcise; they thought no one of them would be left in the Fire. Ibn ʿAbbās in another report: they found written in the Torah that between the two ends of Jahannam lay a forty-year journey up to the tree of Zaqqūm, and, as he said, they imagined that when the count of that journey ran out, Hell itself would come to an end and the punishment cease — whereupon he describes them arriving at the tree of Zaqqūm on the last of the numbered days, and the keepers of Saqar saying: you claimed the Fire would not touch you but a few days — the number has passed and you are in the eternal; then they are driven up the mountain of punishment within Hell. ʿIkrimah reports the confrontation: the Jews disputed with the Prophet ﷺ and said, "We shall enter the Fire only forty nights, and a people after us will succeed us in it" — meaning Muḥammad and his companions; and the Prophet ﷺ put his hand on their heads and said, "Rather, you are in it forever, and no one will succeed you in it" — and the verse came down. Ibn Zayd: the Prophet asked them who the people of the Fire were, and they said, "We, and then you will succeed us," and he answered, "You have lied; you know that we shall not succeed you" [Ṭabarī] [Qurṭubī]. **[Qurṭubī]** records from ʿIkrimah another version of the number: they said the world is seven thousand years, and the people are punished in the Fire one day of the Hereafter for each thousand of this world's years — seven days in all. **[Ibn Kathīr]** and **[Study Quran]** add that the claim is repeated in 3:24, and that it has no warrant in Scripture.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** records from Abū Hurayrah, in a report Abū Bakr b. Mardawayh transmitted and Aḥmad, al-Bukhārī and al-Nasāʾī recorded in similar form, the scene at Khaybar: when the poisoned roast sheep was presented to the Prophet ﷺ, he had all the Jews assembled and asked them, "Who is your father?" "So-and-so." "You have lied — your father is so-and-so." "You have spoken the truth." Then, "Will you tell me the truth if I ask you something?" "Yes, Abū al-Qāsim; and if we lie, you will know it as you knew about our fathers." "Who are the people of the Fire?" "We shall remain in it a short period, and afterwards you will replace us in it." He said, "May you be cursed and humiliated in it! By God, we shall never replace you in it." Then he asked whether they had poisoned the sheep; they said yes; and asked what made them do it, they answered, "We wanted to know whether you were a liar — if you were, we would be rid of you — and if you were a prophet, the poison would not harm you."

**Language & belief.** *A-ttakhadhtum* — the connective *hamzah* is dropped because the interrogative *hamzah* stands in its place [Jalālayn]. **[Qurṭubī]** notes here a juristic byway *(digression)*: he cites *days numbered* to argue against the claim that the word "days" in Arabic cannot cover more than ten, pointing to "three days in Ḥajj" and to "days numbered" designating the whole month of Ramaḍān (2:184) — here it covers forty — and observing that when "days" is annexed to a situation, as in "the days of your walking or your journey," no determinate count is meant. **[Study Quran]** works through why this verse matters for later theology rather than only for polemic: the main body of Islamic theology does hold that believers may be removed from Hell after a term — from 6:128, 11:107 and 78:23, and from the aḥādīth of intercession — and al-Rāzī argues that 2:80 is not necessarily a denial that souls may remain in Hell temporarily: it may be a rebuke of the claim of a specific small number joined with impudent confidence in the absence of any explicit promise; that one cannot assume such forgiveness for all does not prevent an individual being forgiven after a time; and that there is a difference between saying God will not remove a believer from the Fire and saying God never promised to — for God may do what He has not explicitly promised.

**Reflection.** The verse's power is its brevity: two questions, and the boast of a nation is dismantled. The claim to a private covenant was not evidence of humility but its opposite — a claim to a private exemption, a schedule of punishment arranged for the descendants of the men who did the worshipping. Islam's answer to such claims is not that Hell is emptied of those who believe; it is that nobody negotiates the terms of the hereafter with the mere fact of his birth. **[Saʿdī]**'s conclusion follows hard on the verse: they had combined evil-doing with a feeling of security, and the two together are the very definition of the condition the Qurʾān warns against elsewhere — that none feels secure from God's stratagem except the people who are lost (7:99).


## 2:81

*"Nay — whoever earns an evil deed and his sin encompasses him, those are the companions of the Fire, abiding therein forever."*

**Meaning.** The refutation opens with a single word that overturns the claim: *balā* — "no, it is not as you say" [Ṭabarī] [Saʿdī] [Jalālayn]. **[Saʿdī]** then sets down the general law that replaces their private exemption: whoever earns a wrong and is encompassed by his sin, such are the people of the Fire, abiding there. The word *sayyiʾah* is indefinite in a conditional context, so it ranges over idolatry and everything below it; but the intended meaning here is idolatry (*shirk*), because the verse speaks of a sin that *encompasses* its doer and leaves him no way out — which cannot happen to the one who has faith, for his faith keeps his sin from closing over him. And he turns the verse against a party who misused it: the Khawārij argued from it that the Muslim who commits a grave sin disbelieves, and it is in fact a proof against them, since its subject is plainly idolatry. That, he says, is the rule for every falsifier who adduces a verse or a sound ḥadīth for an unsound claim: the proof he cites must turn against him.

**Language — the particle of reply.** **[Ṭabarī]** explains *balā* and its work: it is the affirmation used in every statement that opens with a denial, as *naʿam* is the affirmation to a question that carries no denial; its origin is *bal*, which turns away from a jahd — as in "ʿAmr did not stand, but Zayd did" — and the *yāʾ* was added so that it could carry a pause, since *bal* cannot, being a connective turning back from denial. So the *balā* turns back from the denial, and the *yāʾ* adds the meaning of affirmation [Ṭabarī]. **[Qurṭubī]** gives the grammarians' dispute: Sibawayh held *balā* and *naʿam* are not nouns but particles; the Kufans held *balā* is *bal* with the *yāʾ* appended for the sake of a good pause, the *yāʾ* carrying the sense of affirmation. The practical demonstration they give: if someone asks, "Did you not take a dīnār?" and you answer *naʿam*, you have confirmed the negation — no, I did not take it — while *balā* means "yes, I did take it"; and if a man says "you have no claim on me" and the other says *naʿam*, he affirms — no claim — whereas *balā* rejects the statement: I do have a claim. In the Qurʾān, "Am I not your Lord?" — "Yes (*balā*)" (7:172): had they answered *naʿam* they would have disbelieved [Qurṭubī].

**Views on the verse.** In **[Ṭabarī]**'s report the *sayyiʾah* here is idolatry — Abū Wāʾil, Mujāhid, ʿAṭāʾ, Qatāda, al-Rabīʿ — while al-Suddī said it means sins for which the Fire is promised. **[Ṭabarī]** then states his own method: the wording is general but God intended a particular class of evil in this place, because He assigned the one who earns it and is encompassed *abiding in the Fire forever*, and eternal abiding is for those who disbelieve in God, not for believers — for the reports from the Messenger of God ﷺ are agreed that the people of faith do not remain in it forever. And the pairing settles it: this verse is followed by "those who believe and do righteous deeds — those are the companions of the Garden, abiding in it forever," so that the eternal inhabitants of the Fire are not the eternal inhabitants of the Garden. Ibn ʿAbbās, in Ibn Isḥāq's report, glossed it as: whoever does deeds like yours and disbelieves as you have disbelieved, until his disbelief encompasses whatever good he has. As for what "encompassed by his sin" means, the reports divide: al-Rabīʿ b. Khuthaym — whoever dies before repenting of his wrongs; Abū al-ʿĀliyyah, Mujāhid, al-Ḥasan, Qatāda and al-Rabīʿ b. Anas — major sins; and **[Study Quran]** summarizes: the persistent sinner, the one who dies in his sin unrepented, or the one who commits grave sins and dies in them without turning back. It explains the image with al-Rāzī: the sin overwhelms one's good actions the way one object encircles another, so that the decisive character of the soul is evil; the heart is what is encircled, and only idolatry genuinely deserves eternity because it is a decisive orientation of the heart.

**Ḥadīth & rulings.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** reports from ʿAbd Allāh b. Masʿūd in Aḥmad: "Beware of the belittled sins, for they gather on a man until they destroy him" — and he gave the parable of a people who encamp on open ground and send out for firewood: one man brings a stick, then another, until they have gathered a mass, kindle a fire and cook what they throw into it. **[Qurṭubī]** draws a legal-theoretical point from the double condition: what is suspended on two conditions is not complete with either of them alone — here, earning the evil *and* being encompassed by it — just as "those who say, 'Our Lord is God,' and then stand firm" (41:30) requires both speech and steadfastness, and as the Prophet ﷺ told Sufyān b. ʿAbd Allāh al-Thaqafī, "Say: I believe in God, then be upright" (Muslim). He also notes that Nāfiʿ read *khaṭīʾātuhu* in the plural, the rest in the singular — the plural pointing to multitude, like "if you try to count the favours of God you cannot number them" (14:34).

**Reflection.** The verse answers a claim of privilege with a criterion: not descent, not community, not a ledger of numbered days, but the sin that either passes or closes over a man. **[Saʿdī]**'s warning about proof-texting deserves to stand beside it: the Khawārij read this verse and made of it a doctrine that destroyed communities — and the verse they quoted was about idolatry. The Qurʾān's own habit is to break down the partitions men build around themselves, moving straight from the élite who claim a private covenant to the general rule that separates people by what they do and what their deed does to them.


## 2:82

*"And those who believe and do righteous deeds — those are the companions of the Garden, abiding therein forever."*

**Meaning.** The counterpart of the preceding verse, set directly beside it [Ṭabarī] [Jalālayn]. **[Ṭabarī]** unpacks the two clauses: *believe* is to accept what Muḥammad ﷺ brought; *do righteous deeds* is to obey God — to establish His limits, discharge His obligations and avoid what He has forbidden; and the two verses together are God's own declaration of permanence on both sides, of the Fire and its people and of the Garden and its people, written to refute the Jews who said the Fire would touch them only a few days and that they would then pass into the Garden. Ibn ʿAbbās, in Ibn Isḥāq's report: whoever believes in what you disbelieved and acts on what you abandoned of His religion — theirs is the Garden, abiding; God announces that the recompense of good and of evil stays with its people forever and is never cut off. Ibn Zayd read the verse as naming Muḥammad ﷺ and his companions specifically.

**Belief.** **[Saʿdī]** defines the terms precisely. Faith here means belief in God, His angels, His Books, His messengers and the Last Day; and no deed is *righteous* unless it satisfies two conditions — that it be done purely for God's sake, and that it follow the Sunnah of His Messenger. Then he gives the summary of the two verses: the people of deliverance and success are the people of faith and righteous action, and the ruined are the people of the Fire, those who associate others with God and disbelieve in Him. **[Study Quran]** places the verse in the long debate among the theologians. The mainstream in Islam, both Sunni and Shīʿī, holds that the believer who commits a grave sin remains a believer: evil actions do not invalidate faith, and faith does not preclude punishment for evil actions — while God forgives, no one can know which sins are forgiven, and for whom; those punished may be punished for a period, perhaps a very long one, but the punishment comes to an end. Against that, the Muʿtazilites held that a grave sinner is a *fāsiq* — an intermediate category between believer and disbeliever that nevertheless merits Hell — and the Khawārij that grave sin destroys faith altogether; on the other side the Murjiʾah detached sin from faith so thoroughly that faith alone guaranteed Paradise. It notes that the Qurʾān itself hardly presents the Hereafter as a simple binary, and that the relation of faith to action was thought through more deeply in the Sufi tradition than in the dogmatic schools. **[Maʿārif]** draws the consequence for the argument with the Jews: since Mūsā was not the last prophet but was followed by ʿĪsā and Muḥammad ﷺ, those who denied the later prophets became disbelievers; the claim to be released after a few days therefore collapses, for the law of God on this matter is not suspended by membership in a community.

**Reflection.** The pair of verses is a single sentence divided in two so that no reader can take half of it. Faith without deeds is claimed as a privilege by some; deeds without faith are claimed as a merit by others; and the Qurʾān, having just refused the appeal to lineage, refuses both — belief that works, and work that springs from belief. The two conditions **[Saʿdī]** gives for a righteous deed are the ethical form of the same idea, and they are worth stating as the verse's practical translation: sincerity toward God and conformity to the Prophet's ﷺ example. Anything else may look like religion, and may even feel like it, but it is not what this verse rewards.


## 2:83

*"And when We took a covenant from the Children of Israel: you shall worship none but God, and goodness to parents, and to kin, and to orphans, and to the needy; and speak to people in a goodly way; and establish the prayer and give the alms. Then you turned away, all but a few of you, swerving aside."*

**Meaning.** The Sūrah now sets out the content of the covenant they were charged with and then broke [Jalālayn] [Saʿdī] [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Saʿdī]** opens with a striking observation about their character: every command they were given, they resisted, and would not accept it except under the heaviest oaths and the most binding covenants — here, the covenant was taken before such a programme that a person of insight would recognize it as God's kindness: worship of Him alone as the foundation without which no deed is accepted; then the rights of creation, beginning with the parents; then kin, orphans and the needy; then a universal good that a man can extend to every creature — good speech; then prayer and alms, prayer containing sincerity toward the One worshipped and alms containing goodness to the servants. And after all of it: "you turned away" — a turning of aversion, with no desire and no returning.

**Language.** *Al-mīthāq* is *mifʿāl* from binding oneself by oath and the like [Ṭabarī]. On "you shall worship none but God," the readings divide between the *tāʾ* and the *yāʾ* — both valid, because taking a covenant is a form of adjuration: as one says, "I adjured my brother — you shall surely stand," speaking of him as one present in the words, and "I adjured him, that he should stand," speaking as of an absent man; so with the covenant, the address may be reported in either person [Ṭabarī]. **[Qurṭubī]** adds the grammar: the verb stands in the nominative because the implied *an* was dropped — "We took their covenant that they should not worship any but God, and that they should be good to parents, and not shed blood" — and Ubayy and Ibn Masʿūd read it *lā taʿbudū* as a plain prohibition, which is why the verse rolls on into commands: stand, say, establish, give. ***Wa-qūlū lil-nāsi ḥusnan*** — goodness itself; a variant reading is *ḥasanan*, and *ḥusn* is a verbal noun used as a hyperbolic description. In "and goodness to parents" there is no explicit verb; the sense is "and do goodness to parents," the verb understood [Study Quran]. And *khaṭīʾah*/*ḍalāl* aside, the closing *tawallaytum* carries more than mere departure: the one who turns away may still intend to return, but these had neither desire nor intention of return [Saʿdī].

**Rulings & āthār.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** observes the ordering: God's own right comes first, for He created them for it, as every messenger without exception was sent with "there is no god but I, so worship Me" (21:25) and every community received a messenger with "worship God and shun false deities" (16:36). Then the rights of the created come next, and foremost the parents — which is why God so often joins gratitude to them with gratitude to Him: "give thanks to Me and to your parents; unto Me is the journeying" (31:14). He cites the ḥadīth in the two Ṣaḥīḥs from Ibn Masʿūd: "I asked, 'O Messenger of God, which deed is best?' He said, 'Prayer at its time.' 'Then what?' 'Kindness to parents.' 'Then what?' 'Jihād in God's cause.'" **[Qurṭubī]** gives the practical content of that kindness: living with them in what is right, humility before them, obeying them, praying for forgiveness for them after their death, and keeping ties with those they loved — the details of which belong to Sūrat al-Isrāʾ (17:23-26). He notes why the parents' right is joined here to *tawḥīd*: the first bringing-forth is from God, and the second bringing-forth, the raising of the child, is from the parents' side. **[Study Quran]** adds that these duties hold whether the parents are believers or not, citing the commentators' use of Ibrāhīm's restraint with his disbelieving father (19:42-48), and notes the ḥadīth honouring mothers especially. **[Maʿārif]** puts the social teaching of the verse in a wider frame: the basic elements of religion common to Islam and the earlier laws are *tawḥīd*, service to parents, kin, orphans and the needy, gentle speech with all human beings, and prayer and *zakāh*; and on the command to gentleness he quotes **[Qurṭubī]**'s account: Ṭalḥah b. ʿUmar told ʿAṭāʾ that he was short-tempered with people of unorthodox belief and dealt harshly with them; ʿAṭāʾ said, "Do not do that," recited this verse, and added, "God commanded us to speak to people courteously — and if Jews and Christians are to be treated so, how much more a Muslim, whatever kind of man he is?" He observes that God instructed Mūsā and Hārūn to speak gently to Pharaoh, and no one addressing another today is superior to Mūsā, nor is the one addressed viler than Pharaoh. **[Saʿdī]** draws the same line from the other direction: since a man cannot reach all people with his wealth, he was commanded to the good that he can extend to every creature — speech; and in the command is an implied prohibition of ugly speech to anyone, so that the believer is not obscene, not abusive, not a curser, not a quarreller, but of good character, patient with the harm people bring.

**Reflection.** This verse is a compact constitution, and its order is its argument: God first, then the people nearest to you, then the stranger, and speech as the one currency everyone can pay. **[Maʿārif]**'s story of ʿAṭāʾ is the verse's most practical test: the person hardest to address kindly is usually the one whose beliefs offend us, and the Qurʾān's command to "speak to people a good word" does not carry an exception for him. And the ending is the part of the verse that hurts: after a covenant so reasonable, "you turned away, all but a few of you." The few are mentioned, as **[Saʿdī]** says, so that no one should imagine the whole community turned — mercy keeps a remnant in view.


## 2:84

*"And when We took your covenant: you shall not shed your blood, nor expel your own selves from your homes — then you ratified it, and you bear witness."*

**Meaning.** The second article of the covenant, and the first place in the Sūrah where its violation is named as an event rather than a disposition [Jalālayn] [Saʿdī] [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Saʿdī]** gives the setting at Madīnah: al-Aws and al-Khazraj — the Anṣār — were idolaters before Islam, fighting each other on the old footing; the three Jewish groups, Banū Qurayẓah, Banū al-Naḍīr and Banū Qaynuqāʿ, arrived and each allied itself with one party of the city, so that when the two sides fought, the Jew would aid his ally against the other side's Jews — killing his fellow Jew and driving him out of his home when exile and plunder fell, only to ransom the captives after the fighting stopped. All three things had been made obligatory on them in the covenant: not to shed one another's blood, not to expel one another, and to ransom their captives. They did the last and abandoned the first two — which is the substance of the rebuke that follows in the next verse.

**Language — "your blood," "your selves."** **[Ṭabarī]** puts the question a reader naturally asks — did they use to kill themselves and expel themselves? — and answers: they were forbidden to kill one another, and a man's killing of his brother is his killing of himself, because one religion made them like a single person; he cites the Prophet's ﷺ saying, "The believers in their mutual compassion and affection are like one body: when one limb complains, the whole body answers with fever and sleeplessness." A second account: the man who kills exposes himself to retaliation, so he has caused his own death — one says to the culprit, "You have brought this on yourself." Qatāda: your own self, son of Adam, is the people of your religion and your call; so do not shed their blood. Abū al-ʿĀliyyah: none of you shall kill another nor expel another from his home [Ṭabarī] [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Qurṭubī]** adds the readings — *tusaffikūn* with the vowelled *tāʾ* and doubled *fāʾ*, and the reading with the *fāʾ* vowelled *ḍammah* — and the lexical notes: *al-nafs* comes from *al-nafāsah*, a man's soul being the most precious thing in him; *al-dār* is the dwelling a people settle in, even without buildings, so called because it encircles (*yadūru*) upon its residents as a wall does upon what it contains. *Aqrartum* — you ratified; *wa-antum tashhadūn* — either that you attest to it with your hearts, or that you were present at its taking.

**Rulings & āthār.** **[Qurṭubī]** reads the verse outward, and then applies it inward. Since the passage forbids a man shedding his own blood and exiling himself, Ibn Khuwayz Mindād allowed that the plain sense may be meant as well: a man should not kill himself under hardship and affliction, nor wander in the wilderness refusing to shelter in houses out of a perverse asceticism. And it was reported that ʿUthmān b. Maẓʿūn, with ten companions of the Messenger of God ﷺ, swore an oath to wear rough sackcloth, wander the desert without entering homes, eat no meat and abstain from women; when this reached the Prophet ﷺ he came to ʿUthmān's house, and not finding him, asked his wife about what he had heard — she, unwilling to reveal her husband's secret and unwilling to lie to the Messenger of God ﷺ, said, "If something reached you, it is as it reached you." The Prophet ﷺ said: "Say to ʿUthmān: is this a departure from my Sunnah, or from my religion? I pray and I sleep, I fast and I break the fast, I approach women, I shelter in houses, I eat meat. Whoever turns away from my Sunnah is not of me." And ʿUthmān and his companions returned from what they were on. There follows **[Qurṭubī]**'s own word on it — and it is the sentence that makes this old Israelite covenant sting: "All of this is to be honoured among us too, and all of it has come to pass among us through the tribulations. We belong to God, and to Him we return"; and he cites 6:65, "or He confounds you into sects, and makes you taste the violence of one another." **[Study Quran]** treats the verse as the entry to a longer question — how a community as such can be addressed for the deeds of its ancestors: one may be implicated passively, as children suffer for their parents' crimes; one may also take an active part in them; and where people glory in a group identity and accept its achievements as extensions of themselves, they expose themselves to its crimes as well. It notes the human reality that traits and practices are inherited and imitated across generations — the reason the Qurʾān keeps rebuking the argument "our fathers did so" — while a community may still be rescued from its darkness by an infusion of virtue. **[Maʿārif]** notes simply that this verse supplements the previous one: the pledge was explicit, not a matter of innuendo — as clear as a witness's testimony.

**Reflection.** The verse's phrase "your own selves" is the whole ethical argument in two words. A community bound by one religion is, in the Qurʾān's accounting, one body, and war within it is amputation. **[Qurṭubī]**'s aside about the tribulations among the Muslims, and the story of ʿUthmān b. Maẓʿūn's companions, together make the verse's double edge visible: it forbids the community to devour itself, and it forbids the individual to wage war on himself in the name of piety. Both are violations of the same covenant, and both have a cure in the same sentence: whoever turns away from the Sunnah is not of him.


## 2:85

*"Then it is you, the very ones, who kill your own and expel a party of you from their homes, conspiring against them in sin and enmity. And if they come to you as captives, you ransom them — though their expulsion was forbidden you. Do you then believe in part of the Book and disbelieve in part? What is the requital of those of you who do that but disgrace in the life of this world, and on the Day of Resurrection they shall be consigned to the severest punishment. And God is not heedless of what you do."*

**Meaning.** The covenant is now held up against the conduct, and the absurdity of their practice is set out in a single sentence [Jalālayn] [Saʿdī] [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Jalālayn]** narrates the circle: Qurayẓah was allied with al-Aws and al-Naḍīr with al-Khazraj; when the two Arab sides fought, the Jews fought on both sides, killing and expelling fellow Jews and taking prisoners — and then ransomed the captives. Asked why they fought them, they said, "For fear that our allies be humiliated"; asked why they paid ransoms, they said, "Because we were commanded to ransom." God answers: what is the requital of those who do that but disgrace in this world — and it fell: Qurayẓah were killed, al-Naḍīr expelled to Syria and ordered to pay the *jizyah* — and on the Day of Resurrection, the worst of punishments. **[Saʿdī]** gives the doctrinal point beneath the story: three obligations had been laid on them — not to shed one another's blood, not to expel one another, and to ransom the captive; they kept the last and abandoned the first two, and God's rebuke is the verse's question: do you believe in part of the Book and reject part? And that, he notes, is proof that faith requires performing the commands and avoiding the prohibitions, and that the commanded acts belong to faith itself.

**Context.** Ibn ʿAbbās, in Ibn Isḥāq's report: the Jews went out with the idolaters, shedding their blood alongside them and expelling them alongside them; God reproached them for it, having forbidden the shedding of their blood in the Torah and made obligatory the ransoming of their captives; they made two parties — Banū Qaynuqāʿ allied to al-Khazraj, and al-Naḍīr and Qurayẓah to al-Aws — so that when war broke out, each party backed its allies against its brothers, spilling each other's blood with the Torah in their hands, knowing from it what was owed to them and what was upon them [Ṭabarī] [Ibn Kathīr]. **[Qurṭubī]** notes that this verse is addressed to those who were present and cannot be referred back to their forebears: it came down concerning Banū Qaynuqāʿ, Qurayẓah and al-Naḍīr, and the alliances ran across the community — al-Aws were allies of Qaynuqāʿ and al-Khazraj of Qurayẓah, while al-Naḍīr and Qurayẓah were brothers, until they split and fought; when the fighting stopped, they ransomed their prisoners, and God reproached them for it. **[Maʿārif]** adds the human detail of their excuses: they were asked why they were so diligent about ransoming and answered that releasing prisoners was obligatory for them; and when they were challenged over helping the non-Jews slaughter Jews, they said that failing their friends would be a real disgrace — even if the friends were not Jews. So the verse exposes the duplicity: the Qurʾān labels it *sin and enmity*, which measures two kinds of rights infringed at once — God's right by disobedience, and the creatures' right by the pain and loss inflicted.

**Language.** *Thumma antum hāʾulāʾi* admits two analyses in **[Ṭabarī]**'s account: either *hāʾulāʾi* stands where a vocative was intended — "then you, O these" — the *yāʾ* of address dropped as the context supplies it; or *antum* is the subject and *hāʾulāʾi* an interposed demonstrative with the predicate following, as the Arabs say "I am this one standing." One Baṣran held that *hāʾulāʾi* merely calls attention to and confirms *antum*, comparing "we are these who love them and they love not you" (3:119) and Khufāf b. Nudbah's "I am that one." **[Qurṭubī]** gives the grammarians' alternatives and the readings: the people of Madīnah and Makkah read *taẓẓāharūna* with the *tāʾ* assimilated into the *ẓāʾ*, its origin *tataẓāharūna*; the Kūfans read it lightly, dropping the second *tāʾ* since the first points to it; Qatāda read *taẓharūna*. The root is *al-ẓahr*: mutual backing, each strengthening the other so that he is as a back to him; the poet's line has the image of men who back each other like buttresses against one. *Al-ithm* is the deed that earns blame; *al-ʿudwān* is excess in wrong and overstepping it [Qurṭubī] [Ṭabarī].

**Rulings — believing in part of the Book.** **[Maʿārif]** states the principle: a divine Book must be accepted as a whole; to reject a part of it is to reject it, and that is unbelief. He then faces a difficulty: if the verse rebukes them merely for not *acting* on certain injunctions, how is that called *kufr*, when a believer who accepts the lawfulness of what is lawful and the unlawfulness of what is unlawful in principle is not an unbeliever? His answer: when a sin is very grave, the idiom of the Sharīʿah sometimes calls it *kufr* to underline its seriousness. **[Study Quran]** puts the matter in the larger vocabulary of the Qurʾān: here the wider sense of *kufr* — rejection and denial of certain truths — comes to the fore, since half-belief is not a denial of God's existence but the behaviour of hypocrisy and immorality, which cannot be separated from some degree of denial; it records the view that the inconsistency lies precisely between the expulsion, which violated the Book, and the ransom, which conformed to it, and notes the exegesis that connects the partial unbelief with the prophecies of the Prophet ﷺ, though the context does not imply it.

**Reflection.** The verse is one of the Qurʾān's sharpest portraits of religion used as a menu. They kept the clause that cost them money and gave them prestige — ransoming captives, admired by all — and dropped the clauses that would have cost them tribal loyalty: not to kill, not to expel. God's question — *do you believe in part of the Book and disbelieve in part?* — is not a quibble about jurisprudence. It is the observation that a Book held on those terms is not a revelation at all; it is a mirror for one's preferences, and the tribe, not the Lord, is doing the choosing. And the penalty named is suited to the crime: those who chose the good opinion of their allies over the covenant received, in this world, the loss of standing they feared — Qurayẓah killed, al-Naḍīr exiled, and the *jizyah* imposed — and in the next, a punishment that will not be lifted.


## 2:86

*"Those are they who have bought the life of this world at the price of the Hereafter; so the punishment shall not be lightened for them, nor shall they be helped."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** reads the verse as the naming of the bargain the previous verses described: these are the ones who bought the cheap goods of this world — precedence over the weak and the ignorant of their own community, and coarse food — with the faith that would have brought them, in place of their disbelief, eternal dwelling in the Gardens; they were content with this world through their unbelief as the price of the Hereafter's bliss, and so had no share in it at all. Qatāda put it plainly: they preferred the little of this world to the much of the Hereafter. And the sentence about them follows from the bargain: whoever has no share in the Hereafter's bliss has only the punishment there, and for those there is no alleviation and no interval — because it is only the one who has some share in bliss for whom punishment might be lightened. As for "nor shall they be helped," **[Ṭabarī]** explains it as a denial of every kind of help: no one will fend off God's punishment from them, by power, by intercession, or by anything else [Ṭabarī] [Maʿārif]. **[Jalālayn]** gives the verse's own definition of the exchange: they bought this world *by preferring it* to the next — the preference is the purchase.

**Rulings.** In **[Qurṭubī]**'s reading the passage yields a standing legal obligation. His scholars say: the ransoming of captives is obligatory even if not a single dirham remains. Ibn Khuwayz Mindād held that the verse contains the obligation of freeing prisoners; the reports from the Prophet ﷺ show that he freed captives and commanded their freeing; the practice of the Muslims ran on it and consensus settled upon it. It is owed from the public treasury, and if there is none, it is an obligation on all the Muslims collectively, which whoever carries it out discharges for the rest. And then he wrote the sentence that has kept this commentary alive: "By God, we too have turned away from all of it, through the tribulations — each of us backing himself with others against the others! Would that it had been against the disbelievers; but no — against the Muslims, indeed not the disbelievers! — until we left our brothers humiliated and abased, the rule of the polytheists running over them. There is no might and no power except with God, the Most High, the Mighty." **[Ibn Kathīr]** draws the standing conclusion the passage is aimed at: since these people implemented the Torah when it suited them and defied it when it did not — knowing the while that they were wrong — they can be trusted neither to preserve it nor to convey it; and they are not to be believed in what they relate of the description of the Messenger of God ﷺ, his coming, his expulsion from his land and his migration, all of which the earlier prophets had told them and which they hid.

**Reflection.** The verse gives the conduct of the previous passage its true name: not politics, not loyalty, not honour — a sale. And it is worth noticing what was on each side of the scale in **[Ṭabarī]**'s account, because he refuses to romanticize the motive: what they purchased was precedence over the weak-witted among their own people and coarse food. Rank over the vulnerable, and a meal, bought with the Gardens. Every generation of believers has the same market open in front of it, and the only mercy is that the terms are stated in advance: the price is this world, and the merchandise is the next.
